Lab curves at equal tokens: the measured gain is time

Four sources, and their methods differ: OpenAI's charts for GPT-5.6 Sol with 1, 4 and 16 agents; Toby Ord's (Oxford) recasting of those points into the labour-economics parameter λ; and the system cards of Claude Opus 5 and Opus 5.5, which set a single agent against teams of 5 and 10 and report teams of 1–100 agents by best score after 2, 6 and 24 hours. As of 3 October 2026 we found no publications with swarm measurements from DeepSeek, Qwen (Alibaba), Zhipu (GLM), MiniMax or Mistral. The exception among labs of this group is Moonshot AI, with the swarm mode in Kimi, below, in the section on reinforcement learning.

What λ is (the "stepping on each other's toes" effect)

Toby Ord carried a parameter from labour economics over to agent swarms. N workers get as much done as one worker working Nλ times as long. With λ = 1 the task divides with no losses. With λ = 0.5 a hundred agents equal one agent working 10 times as long. On a plot of quality against total tokens (log axis) Ord drew a swarm line: from 1 to 4 to 16 agents at fixed tokens per agent. Its slope relative to the "one agent thinks longer" line is λ. Three rules follow from λ. A swarm 10 times larger gives as much as a single agent thinking 10λ ≈ 3–5 times longer. To match a 100-fold longer chain of reasoning in a single agent, the swarm must grow 900–15,000 times. N agents can finish roughly Nλ times faster at N1−λ times the spend: with λ ≈ 0.5, sixteen agents give about a quarter of the time for four times the cost. All values come from one model, one architecture (a lead agent plus subagents) and at most 16 agents. Swarms of thousands lack an equal-token single-agent comparison.

Benchmark (OpenAI data, GPT-5.6 Sol) λ 90% interval Swarm sizes
BrowseComp 0.68 0.63–0.76 1, 4, 16 agents
SEC-Bench Pro 0.57 0.52–0.61 1, 4, 16 agents
Terminal-Bench 2.1 0.48 0.40–0.57 only 1 and 4 agents

⟷ scroll the table sideways

The λ values in the table were obtained by Ord (Claude Opus 5 ran the regressions at his request), not by OpenAI; our recalculation from the data embedded in the page reproduces them.

theory / essayeconomics & scaletheoryThird-party OpenAI data up to 16 agents; no agent runs of his own

Swarm Scaling

University of Oxford; Forethought · Sep 2026

From the points of OpenAI's GPT-5.6 Sol charts, Ord estimated λ = 0.48–0.68: at equal tokens one agent beats a swarm, and a swarm of N agents equals a single agent thinking Nλ times longer.

×
Fig. 4.1original ↗
More about this work
Authors
Toby Ord
Organizations
University of Oxford; Forethought
Published
21 Sep 2026
Status
Personal blog post (LessWrong, EA Forum)
Source
lesswrong.com/posts/6cb7qd3RSkgnviCpf/swarm-scaling

Ord (post of 21 September 2026) took the interactive charts from the GPT-5.6 Sol launch. One model solves BrowseComp, SEC-Bench Pro and Terminal-Bench 2.1 alone, as four agents, and, on the first two, as sixteen, at five effort levels. He replotted the points on a log scale of total output tokens across all agents and drew a "swarm line" at fixed tokens per agent. The slope of that line relative to the "one agent thinks longer" line is λ. The regressions were run by Claude Opus 5.

  • A 4-agent swarm needs about twice as many tokens as a single agent for the same result, and a 16-agent swarm about twice as many again.
  • λ is 0.68 on BrowseComp, 0.57 on SEC-Bench Pro and 0.48 on Terminal-Bench 2.1. Only 1 and 4 agents appear on the last of these. A swarm 16 times larger equals a single agent thinking 160.57 ≈ 4.9 times longer.
  • Time and money below are our estimate, not Ord's. From OpenAI's simulated latencies at equal quality, 16 agents are 2–2.8 times faster than a single agent on SEC-Bench Pro and 3.8–4.6 times faster on BrowseComp, while 4 agents on Terminal-Bench 2.1 are 1.1–1.4 times faster. In dollars at API prices λ comes out higher: 0.54–0.71.

The estimate rests on the lab's own chart points. At equal tokens a single agent beats a swarm, and the swarm's main benefit is time. Ord allows that a large swarm's ceiling may turn out higher than a single agent's, but this has not yet been shown. The closest example, and an expensive one, is the Claude Opus 5 team on BrowseComp, below.

This is a blog post, not a paper. The regressions, whose specification is unpublished, use 5 points per swarm size, on one model and one rigid "lead plus subagents" architecture. OpenAI does not disclose the number of tasks or the variance. Latency and cost are an offline simulation, and the runs are far from the context limit, so memory effects (Chapter 6) are not in the data. The author removed the section on the 10,000-agent swarm and Navier–Stokes on 27 September 2026. According to OpenAI staff, as relayed by two commenters, the chart showed a single agent's reasoning getting longer, not the swarm getting larger. OpenAI has not clarified this officially.

engineering reporteconomics & scaleplatforms & simulatorsUp to 16 agents in the published charts

GPT-5.6: Frontier intelligence that scales with your ambition

OpenAI · Jul 2026

GPT-5.6 Sol launch: the 4-agent Ultra mode lifts BrowseComp from 90.4% to 92.2% at the cost of more tokens and money; chart points for 1, 4 and 16 agents are embedded in the page.

More about this work
Authors
OpenAI
Organizations
OpenAI
Published
9 Jul 2026
Status
Lab blog
Source
openai.com/index/gpt-5-6/

In the GPT-5.6 Sol launch post of 9 July 2026, OpenAI described the Ultra mode. By default four agents work in parallel, and a beta API lets developers launch subagents and synthesize their work in a single request. The interactive charts compare 1 agent, 4 (Ultra) and, for BrowseComp and SEC-Bench Pro, 16 agents at five effort levels. The axes are total output tokens of all agents, simulated cost at API prices, and simulated latency of the root agent.

The launch table publishes only Sol/Ultra pairs: BrowseComp 90.4% → 92.2%, SEC-Bench Pro 71.2% → 74.3%, Terminal-Bench 2.1 88.8% → 91.9%. The gain comes with more tokens and higher cost. The chart points themselves, including the 16-agent ones, are embedded in the page as Vega-Lite data, and Ord worked from them. The developer guide adds that GPT-5.6 was trained "end-to-end" with "native multi-agent orchestration". Instructing the model on when to invoke subagents makes it more likely to spawn them only where the extra tokens would improve the result.

At equal total tokens these data show no swarm advantage (see Ord's card). The launch shows parallelism paying off in speed and a few points of quality. This is one of the few public datasets in which a frontier model is systematically run as a swarm of three sizes, and OpenAI's first product in which multi-agent operation is built into training rather than only into the harness.

The post is marketing, without peer review, and it gives no count of tasks, runs or confidence intervals. Latency and cost are an offline simulation based on production data, and OpenAI itself notes that real results may differ substantially. Latency is measured at the root agent and hides subagent queues, and the architecture is a fixed "root plus subagents" hierarchy, not a free swarm.

theory / essayeconomics & scaletheoryin the wildThe interview recounts others' runs; no agent experiment of its own

Noam Brown – Agent swarms, alignment, & recursive self-improvement

Dwarkesh Podcast; OpenAI · Sep 2026

Noam Brown (OpenAI): four agents finish roughly twice as fast at twice the cost; for the Navier–Stokes result he would not give the swarm even 10% of the credit.

More
Authors
Dwarkesh Patel, Noam Brown
Organizations
Dwarkesh Podcast; OpenAI
Published
17 Sep 2026
Status
Podcast (interview)
Source
dwarkesh.com/p/noam-brown

Noam Brown (OpenAI), interviewed by Dwarkesh Patel on 17 September 2026, described swarm economics in words: on some benchmarks, "if you have four agents working on the problem, it is done twice as fast … you're paying 2x more to get an answer twice as quickly"; with 16 agents the picture is similar but slightly less efficient, and the speedup is "slightly sublinear, though it does depend a lot on the problem" (search and mathematics parallelize better than writing a novel). OpenAI's public measurements go up to 16 agents; for the swarm of about 10,000 agents that, according to OpenAI, solved the Navier–Stokes problem (Chapter 2), there is no single-agent control run ("we haven't done that experiment yet"), and Brown says "I wouldn't even attribute 10% of the credit to multi-agent", although "we think it helped". An interview, not data: no benchmark names, tokens or intervals.

system cardeconomics & scaleself-organizationsafetyTeams of up to 100 agents; 24-hour attempts

System Card: Claude Opus 5.5 — multi-agent sections (§8.12 and others)

Anthropic · Sep 2026

Opus 5.5 system card: on ProgramBench a team of 5 reaches the same level 2.7 times faster at ≈1.9× tokens; over 24 hours almost all the gain comes from going from 1 to 10 agents.

×
Fig. 4.2original ↗
×
Fig. 4.3original ↗
More about this work
Authors
Anthropic
Organizations
Anthropic
Published
22 Sep 2026
Status
Model system card
Source
www-cdn.anthropic.com/fc1b44717c85dc068bc6ba5024219938094694bd/Claude%20Opus%2…

Section 8.12 of the Claude Opus 5.5 system card (22 September 2026) covers teams made of copies of the model. Small teams: a single agent versus a team of 5 peer agents and versus a lead with subagents on ProgramBench (166 tasks: reconstruct a program's source from its binary and documentation) and DRACO (100 short research tasks). Large teams of 1, 10, 30 and 100 agents work for 24 hours without network access to build a knowledge base about a company and to prove a theorem in Lean, with three attempts per point and a comparison against Opus 5 and Fable 5.1.

  • On ProgramBench a team of 5 reaches 60% of hidden tests 2.7 times faster than a single agent but spends about 1.9 times as many tokens (our estimate from the chart). All three architectures share the same ceiling, around 97–98%.
  • On DRACO, without limits, teams are slower than a single agent because of coordination. At a time budget of 0.5× the single-agent latency on the same task, a team of 5 scores no less than an unlimited single agent, about 2.8 times faster.
  • Over 24 hours the knowledge base (share of hidden questions a reader answers correctly from the team's notes alone) is 0.53 / 0.70 / 0.71 / 0.74 for 1 / 10 / 30 / 100 agents. Lean: 0.39 / 0.66 / 0.66 / 0.68. Almost all of the gain comes from going from 1 to 10.
  • 10 Opus 5.5 agents in Lean (0.66) beat 100 agents of Opus 5 (0.58) and Fable 5.1 (0.53).
  • After 2 hours, 100 agents score 0.44 in Lean, a single agent 0.07.

This is a quality-versus-tokens curve for a frontier-model team from the developer itself. The team buys time with tokens, the final ProgramBench level is the same, returns to team size fall off quickly, and a new model generation gives more than a tenfold larger team. The large teams are compared at equal time, not equal cost (Chapters 5 and 6).

This is a self-report without peer review. The large teams ran on internal tasks and on "a slightly different model version than the released one", and multi-agent ProgramBench ran on "an alternate, broadly comparable snapshot". Confidence intervals exist only for ProgramBench, 166 tasks, with 95% bands from between-task variance. The large teams have two tasks, three attempts per point and no intervals at all, and DRACO is a mean of two runs. Tokens and dollars for the 24-hour runs are not disclosed, "time" is computed from tokens, and the single agent had not plateaued after 24 hours.

system cardeconomics & scalememoryplatforms & simulatorsFixed team of up to 10 copies; async subagent count not limited

System Card: Claude Opus 5 — §8.11 Multi-Agent

Anthropic · Jul 2026

Opus 5 system card: a team of 10 agents scores 93.6% on BrowseComp versus 90.5% for the best single agent at an equal nominal token budget, but at roughly 2.5 times the cost.

×
Fig. 4.4original ↗
More about this work
Authors
Anthropic
Organizations
Anthropic
Published
24 Jul 2026
Status
Model system card
Source
www-cdn.anthropic.com/b514064af1408018e64b1ad24e7d5e75850b4ffd/Claude%20Opus%2…

In Section 8.11 of the Claude Opus 5 system card (24 July 2026), Anthropic compared one agent with teams of model copies on two benchmarks: BrowseComp (find a hard-to-find fact on the web) and ProgramBench. Architectures: 5 or 10 peer agents with messaging and a lead, or a lead with asynchronous helpers. Each agent had a budget of 1 million tokens. The single agent in search got 1, 3 or 10 million with history compaction. Time is computed, and cost is at API prices with perfect caching.

  • On BrowseComp all team architectures are no worse than the best single agent. The team of 10 agents gave the best result: 93.6% versus 90.5% for a single agent with a 10-million-token budget (+3.1 points). Raising that agent's budget from 3 to 10 million added 0.7 points.
  • The gain came at an equal nominal token budget (10 × 1 million versus 10 million for the best single agent) but at roughly 2.5 times the actual spend. In Anthropic's words, teams shift the quality–cost frontier "up and to the right". From the chart, a task costs the 10-agent team about $95 versus $37. Going from 5 to 10 agents added 0.9 points at about 1.6 times the cost.
  • On ProgramBench a team of 5 reaches 60% of hidden tests 2.2 times faster than a single agent while spending about 2.5 times as many tokens. Its higher final score is explained mainly by a five times larger total budget (the single agent had 1 million tokens).

In search, the team raises the ceiling, not only the speed. It goes above a level the single agent did not reach at any budget tried, and pays for this in money. In programming, the team buys only speed. In the Opus 5.5 card the single agent was given 20 million tokens, the final levels converged, and the speedup grew to 2.7 times. Both the model and the budgets changed.

This is a pre-release model version with unpublished settings and without safeguard classifiers, and the authors call the numbers relative. For BrowseComp, the number of questions, runs and confidence intervals is not given, while ProgramBench has 166 tasks and 95% bands from between-task variance. BrowseComp tasks with leakage found after the fact were scored as wrong, and how many of them is not stated. Prices and tokens not in the card's text are our estimates from the charts.

engineering reporteconomics & scaleplatforms & simulatorsself-organization1,173 distinct agents edited one file in a run; parallel maximum unknown

Agent swarms and the new model economics

Cursor (Anysphere) · Jul 2026

Cursor: a swarm wrote SQLite in Rust for $1,339 with a model mix versus $10,565 with GPT-5.5 in every role, with similar results; there is no single-agent control.

More
Authors
Wilson Lin
Organizations
Cursor (Anysphere)
Published
20 Jul 2026
Status
Company blog
Source
cursor.com/blog/agent-swarm-model-economics

Cursor (Wilson Lin, blog post of 20 July 2026) gave its swarm the task of writing SQLite in Rust from the 835-page manual, without source code or internet access. The comparison is the new swarm against the old one, same models, four-hour limit. The design of both versions and the flat swarm's failure are in Chapter 5. By the fourth hour the new runs passed 73–85% of sqllogictest tests versus 11–77% for the old ones, and later reached 100%. The price is set by the model mix. The post does not disclose the number of agents. $1,339 for an Opus 4.8 planner with Composer 2.5 workers, versus $10,565 for GPT-5.5 in every role, with similar results: almost an eightfold price difference for the same outcome. There is no "one long agent" control, and there is one run per configuration. "Context efficiency, more than parallelism" is the author's hypothesis (Chapter 6).

Controlled comparisons at equal budget

In these studies the team and the single agent get the same tasks, the same tools and a shared token budget that the team splits among its members; actual spend may differ. They fill the gap in the lab data, where swarm and single agent rarely get the same budget. The team's effect is set mainly by the task: the team wins where the task naturally splits into parts and loses where each step depends on the previous one; a mean effect of zero hides a wide spread (Kim et al. card). The largest of these comparisons, 260 configurations, is peer-reviewed in Nature Machine Intelligence.

benchmark / dataseteconomics & scaleself-organizationplatforms & simulatorsSizes 1–9; baseline system of 3 agents

Capable language models can outgrow the benefits of collaboration (preprint: “Towards a Science of Scaling Agent Systems”)

Google Research; Google DeepMind; MIT · Dec 2025

260 configurations under a shared token budget: the team's mean effect is 0.0% with a 95% CI of −58.7…+77.2%; the sign is set by task and architecture (from +80.8% to −70.0%).

×
Fig. 4.5original ↗
×
Fig. 4.6original ↗
More about this work
Authors
Yubin Kim, Ken Gu, Chanwoo Park, Chunjong Park, Samuel Schmidgall et al. (20 authors)
Organizations
Google Research; Google DeepMind; MIT
Published
9 Dec 2025
Status
Nature Machine Intelligence (peer-reviewed journal)
Source
doi.org/10.1038/s42256-026-01268-y

Google Research, Google DeepMind and MIT (Yubin Kim and co-authors) compared one agent with three-agent teams in four architectures: independent workers, a coordinator, peers with voting, a hybrid. The tasks, the tools and the shared token budget were the same. The team split that budget among its members. In total, 260 configurations: six agentic benchmarks (web search, financial analysis, planning in Minecraft, office tasks, code repair, terminal) and nine 2025 models from GPT-5-nano to GPT-5, Gemini 2.5 Pro and Claude Sonnet 4.5, eight of them on code repair and terminal. The preprint appeared on 9 December 2025, the peer-reviewed version in Nature Machine Intelligence on 24 July 2026.

  • The team's mean gain over a single agent across 6 benchmarks and 4 architectures is 0.0%, with a 95% CI from −58.7 to +77.2%. The range runs from +80.8% (financial analysis with a coordinator: 63.1% vs 34.9%) to −70.0% (step-by-step planning in PlanCraft with independent agents: 17.0% vs 56.8%). On code repair, every team is worse than the single agent.
  • The rule that extra agents will not help once a single agent solves more than ~45% of tasks predicted the sign of the effect in 15 of 16 held-out cases. The authors call it a practical selection rule, not a law.
  • Reasoning steps rise by 58% (independent), 263% (peers), 285% (coordinator) and 515% (hybrid). Successes per 1,000 tokens are 2.8–5 times lower for the three architectures with message exchange: 23.9 / 21.5 / 13.6 versus 67.7.
  • Without a verifying component, errors multiply. At the level of logs, independent agents amplify them 17.2 times. In final answers the difference is about 1.1 times.

This is a peer-reviewed comparison at equal compute budgets, not at equal actual token spend. The zero mean here means heterogeneity, not absence of an effect. On individual tasks the team wins or loses by a lot. The sign is set mainly by the task and by whether the architecture fits it. Hence a selection rule: the better a single agent handles the task alone, the less sense a team makes.

The budget is small, about 4,800 reasoning tokens per task, with teams of three and up to nine in one experiment, models from 2025, and predefined architectures only. On SWE-bench and Terminal-Bench there are 20 tasks per cell (about ±20 pp) and one run per configuration, and only the single-agent result passes a strict statistical test.

experiment with LLM agentseconomics & scalememory4 parallel workers, then an aggregator; not a persistent swarm

Single-Agent LLMs Outperform Multi-Agent Systems on Multi-Hop Reasoning Under Equal Thinking Token Budgets

Stanford University · Apr 2026

At the same reasoning-token budget (500 and above), one agent is on average no worse than the best of five multi-agent architectures on the multi-hop questions of FRAMES and MuSiQue.

More
Authors
Dat Tran, Douwe Kiela
Organizations
Stanford University
Published
2 Apr 2026
Status
arXiv preprint (under review)
Source
arxiv.org/abs/2604.02460

Dat Tran and Douwe Kiela (Stanford, preprint of 2 April 2026) compared one agent with five multi-agent architectures: sequential, parallel subtasks, roles, debate, ensemble. The reasoning-token budget is the same, from 100 to 10,000, on the multi-hop questions of FRAMES and MuSiQue, with the models Qwen3, DeepSeek-R1-Distill and Gemini 2.5. At any budget from 500 up, the single agent is on average no worse than the best architecture: 0.418 vs 0.388 at a budget of 1,000, and 0.426 vs 0.423 at 10,000. On open models it actually spends fewer tokens: 1.3 thousand versus 1.8–5.6 thousand for Qwen3-30B on FRAMES at a budget of 10,000. Exceptions: at a budget of 100 the architectures lead, and with Gemini-2.5-Pro on MuSiQue debate is better by 0.03–0.05. The architectures are 2–5 calls, the questions are text-only, the models are from 2025. This does not test a swarm of dozens of agents.

The Illusion of Multi-Agent Advantage

Salesforce Research; HKUST (Guangzhou); UBC; Nanyang Technological University · Jun 2026

Six automatically built multi-agent systems fail to beat voting over five reasoning chains in 10 of 18 dataset–model pairs, at a median cost 1.7 times higher.

More
Authors
Prathyusha Jwalapuram, Hehai Lin, Chuyuan Li, Fangkai Jiao, Sudong Wang et al. (10 authors)
Organizations
Salesforce Research; HKUST (Guangzhou); UBC; Nanyang Technological University
Published
11 Jun 2026
Status
arXiv preprint
Source
arxiv.org/abs/2606.13003

Salesforce Research with HKUST (Guangzhou), UBC and NTU (preprint of 11 June 2026) compared six automatically built multi-agent systems (DyLAN, MAS-Zero, ADAS, AFlow, MaAS, MAS-Orchestra) with voting over five reasoning chains on GPQA-Diamond, HLE-Maths, SWE-Bench Lite, BrowseComp-Plus and their own SMFR task. In 10 of 18 dataset–model pairs no system beats voting (including all four on BrowseComp-Plus); the median cost is 1.7 times higher, and for 14% of pairs 10 times or more. Agents in the generated systems converge immediately (with GPT-5, in more than 90% of DyLAN cases), and architecture search often rediscovers self-consistency. Manual decomposition on SMFR lifts GPT-5 from 57.0% to 96.5% at 1.16 times the cost: structure helps when a human builds it for the task. Decentralized teams are excluded.

experiment with LLM agentsecho & diversitybeliefs & consensus5 agents simultaneously; ablations with N=1–5

Debate or Vote: Which Yields Better Decisions in Multi-Agent Large Language Models?

University of Wisconsin-Madison · Aug 2025

Voting over five independent answers without discussion is more accurate than multi-agent debate (0.769 vs 0.738 for Qwen2.5-7B); the benefit comes from the number of independent attempts, not from communication.

More
Authors
Hyeong Kyu Choi, Xiaojin Zhu, Sharon Li
Organizations
University of Wisconsin-Madison
Published
24 Aug 2025
Status
arXiv preprint; NeurIPS 2025 (Spotlight)
Source
arxiv.org/abs/2508.17536

The University of Wisconsin–Madison (NeurIPS 2025, spotlight; preprint August 2025) split "multi-agent debate" into two parts: five independent answers with voting, and rounds of discussion. On seven task sets, for Qwen2.5-7B voting without discussion gives 0.769, the best debate variant 0.738 and a single agent 0.721; for Llama3.1-8B, 0.724 / 0.699 / 0.620; centralized debate is worse than a single agent, and extra rounds do not help. For an idealized model, the authors prove that the expected belief in the correct answer does not change during discussion (a martingale): the benefit comes from the number of independent attempts, not from communication. 2024 models, short answers, identical agents; for current swarms this gives theory, not a measurement.

Language Model Teams as Distributed Systems

Princeton University; MIT; University of Cambridge; New York University · Mar 2026

Teams of 1–5 agents get a speedup from external task assignment (best cell: 3.35× in time at 1.33× tokens); when agents claim tasks themselves, the team is slower than a single agent.

More
Authors
Elizabeth Mieczkowski, Katherine M. Collins, Ilia Sucholutsky, Natalia Vélez, Thomas L. Griffiths
Organizations
Princeton University; MIT; University of Cambridge; New York University
Published
12 Mar 2026
Status
arXiv preprint
Source
arxiv.org/abs/2603.12229

Princeton (Mieczkowski, Griffiths and co-authors; Collins also at MIT and Cambridge, Sucholutsky at NYU; preprint of 12 March 2026): teams of 1–5 identical agents (Claude Sonnet 4.6, Gemini 3 Flash, GPT-5.2) work on tasks of 20 functions each, with a set share of parallel dependencies. With tasks assigned from outside, the speedup follows the shape of Amdahl's law but stays below the bound for the pooled models; the best cell (Claude, parallel structure, 5 agents) gives 3.35× in time at 1.33× tokens. When agents claim tasks themselves, the median speedup is 0.88× versus 1.36×, i.e. the team is slower than a single agent, and the median number of failed tests is 19 versus 4. On sequential work, tokens grow 5.83 times for a 1.13× speedup. Small homogeneous teams, self-reported success.

How we built our multi-agent research system (2025) and When to use multi-agent systems (2026)

Anthropic · Jun 2025

Anthropic: the Research system gave +90.2% over a single Opus 4 at ≈15× chat tokens; the 2026 advice: multi-agent setups use 3–10 times more tokens and buy thoroughness, not speed.

More
Authors
Jeremy Hadfield, Barry Zhang, Kenneth Lien, Florian Scholz, Jeremy Fox et al. (11 authors)
Organizations
Anthropic
Published
13 Jun 2025
Status
Two company blog posts
Source
anthropic.com/engineering/multi-agent-research-system

In June 2025, Anthropic engineers described the Research system: a lead Claude Opus 4 launches 3–5 Claude Sonnet 4 subagents with separate context windows. On an internal evaluation this gave +90.2% over a single Opus 4, but multi-agent runs use about 15× the tokens of an ordinary chat, and on BrowseComp token volume alone explains 80% of the variance in results. In January 2026 the vendor's advice narrowed: in their testing, multi-agent implementations use 3–10 times more tokens than a single agent and "often take longer overall"; the main benefit of parallelism is thoroughness of coverage, not speed; teams spent months building complex systems and then matched them by improving a single agent's prompt. Engineering blog posts without sample sizes, intervals or an equal-budget control; the two token figures use different baselines.

experiment with LLM agentseconomics & scalememoryplatforms & simulators8 independent parallel runs, followed by a single aggregator

Agentic Aggregation for Parallel Scaling of Long-Horizon Agentic Tasks

Princeton University · Apr 2026

Eight independent runs plus a trajectory "reader": for GLM-4.7-Flash on BrowseComp, 56.00% versus 27.42% for one run, but all eight runs are paid for in full.

More
Authors
Yoonsang Lee, Howard Yen, Xi Ye, Danqi Chen
Organizations
Princeton University
Published
13 Apr 2026
Status
arXiv preprint; accepted at COLM 2026
Source
arxiv.org/abs/2604.11753

Princeton Language and Intelligence (Lee, Yen, Ye, Chen; preprint of 13 April 2026, accepted at COLM 2026): one agent is run eight times independently, then a separate "reader" analyses the finished trajectories. For GLM-4.7-Flash on BrowseComp a single run gives 27.42%, at least one of eight 58.67%, the reader 56.00%; on HLE the ceiling is 50.97% and the reader 37.42%. Averaged over six benchmarks, 47.90 versus 42.58 for the previous way of merging answers. The reader is cheap (a few percent on top of the runs), but the eight runs are still paid for; beating "best of eight" happened only when the stronger MiniMax read GLM's traces on one benchmark (72.67 vs 72.00). Parallel attempts plus a reader, not a communicating team: a clear view of the price of width.

Where communication raises final quality

Almost all of the controlled comparisons above either forbid agents to communicate while working or give them short tasks with nothing to divide. In September 2026 Microsoft Research ran the missing experiment: the same agents, the same budget per agent, a shared folder for findings, and tasks where progress is measurable step by step. Communication raises the final result, not only speed, when the intermediate result is verifiable and the budget is sufficient. Similar results from other chapters — DeLM and CORAL (Chapter 6), Self-Organizing Agent Teams (Chapter 5) — are collected in the table at the start of this chapter.

experiment with LLM agentsself-organizationeconomics & scalememoryTeams of 2–5 agents in one container

Scaling Discovery through Test-Time Communication

Microsoft Research; UC Berkeley · Sep 2026

With the same number of agents, a team of 5 with a shared folder solves 8.0% of ARC-AGI-3 games versus 2.2% for the best of five independent runs (best@5).

×
Fig. 4.7original ↗
×
Fig. 4.8original ↗
More about this work
Authors
Jongho Park, Vasilis Kontonis, Shivam Garg, Akshay Krishnamurthy, Dimitris Papailiopoulos
Organizations
Microsoft Research; UC Berkeley
Published
17 Sep 2026
Status
arXiv preprint
Source
arxiv.org/abs/2609.21032

Microsoft Research (AI Frontiers) with UC Berkeley (Park, Papailiopoulos and co-authors; preprint of 17 September 2026) contrasted two ways of spending k times more compute: run k agents independently and take the best result (best@k), or let the same k agents work simultaneously in one container, sharing findings through a shared folder of logs, with no roles and no boss. Tasks: 25 ARC-AGI-3 games (Claude Sonnet 4.6; 64 single-agent runs per game and 20 team runs per team size), polyomino packing from Frontier-CS, MNIST classifier compression, Terminal-Bench 2.0.

  • On ARC-AGI-3 a team of 3 solves 4.6% of games versus 1.4% for best@3, and a team of 5 solves 8.0% versus 2.2% (a single agent about 0.5%). Catching up takes 13 and 33 independent agents. None of the 64 single-agent runs beat the LP85 game. A team of 5 beats it in 65% of runs.
  • The team spends more output tokens but reaches a given level sooner. Independent agents need 3.8–4.9 times more tokens to match its final result. At a small budget (under ~400 thousand tokens per agent) the independent agents lead. On two games a team of 5 also beat a single agent with five times the action budget, while a team squeezed into a single agent's budget lost.
  • Polyomino in 3 hours: the best team run scores 0.945 versus 0.883 for the best single run and 0.894 for the previous record.
  • MNIST: a team of 4 GPT-5.6 Sol agents compressed the classifier to 1,957 bytes at 99.41% accuracy, versus 3,160 for the best single run.

This is a controlled comparison with the same number of agents and the same action budget per agent. The team with a shared folder beats the best of the independent runs on final quality, not only on speed, although it spends more output tokens, and the conditions are narrow: the gain appears where intermediate progress is measurable and the budget is sufficient.

This is a preprint. The absolute ARC numbers are tiny, and the gain rests on 4 of 25 games. For polyomino and MNIST the best runs are shown, and there is a single MNIST team; only output tokens are counted. On Terminal-Bench 2.0, where progress is hard to verify, a team of two did not beat two independent agents, and the model is deliberately not the strongest.

Accelerating scientific discovery with Co-Scientist

Google DeepMind; Google Research; Stanford; Imperial College London; Houston Methodist · Feb 2025

Google's Co-Scientist: the system's own Elo for its best hypotheses keeps rising with compute without saturating, and blinded experts give it a mean rank of 2.36 out of four; the architecture is human-designed.

More
Authors
Juraj Gottweis, Wei-Hung Weng, Alexander Daryin, Tao Tu, Petar Sirkovic, Artiom Myaskovsky et al. (51 authors)
Organizations
Google DeepMind; Google Research; Stanford; Imperial College London; Houston Methodist
Published
26 Feb 2025
Status
Nature (peer-reviewed journal)
Source
doi.org/10.1038/s41586-026-10644-y

Google's Co-Scientist (Gottweis, Weng and 49 other authors from Google Cloud AI Research, Google DeepMind, Google Research, Stanford, Imperial College and others; preprint February 2025, Nature online 19 May 2026): a supervisor and six roles built on Gemini 2.0 generate, critique and rank hypotheses in a tournament. On 203 goals the system's own Elo for its best hypotheses rises with compute with no sign of saturation; on 11 goals, blinded experts give the system a mean rank of 2.36 out of four (1 = best). Wet-lab checks are limited to a few spot tests: KIRA6 with an IC50 of 10 nM on the KG-1a line versus 180 nM on the control. A follow-up (preprint of 27 August 2026): grossly fabricated results were found in 4% of the full system's autonomous papers, in 46% for its version without the reliability modules, and in 90% for another system, Agent Laboratory. Elo is the system's own scale; the architecture is human-designed.

High-profile campaigns without a control run

High-profile 2026 results produced by swarms were not compared with a single agent. OpenAI's swarm of about 10,000 agents and the Navier–Stokes problem are described in Chapter 2; there is no control run, and OpenAI itself does not regard the swarm as the main reason for the success (Brown's card above). Anthropic's campaigns (the zeta function and cryptography below; Fermat's Last Theorem and the ART enzyme search in Chapter 6, where "about 950 agents" means 949 short sessions with at most 58 running at once) and the Purdue work follow the same pattern: a big result, a large token spend and no test of whether a swarm was needed. The incidents of summer 2026 (Chapter 3) add two opposite observations, each from a single episode and without a comparison on quality and tokens: Ajeya Cotra of METR believes that in the July attack on Hugging Face the collective of agents on a shared board did what even a very long-lived single agent could not have done, while an independent reconstruction of a separate, earlier wiki episode (Lütje) finds no robust link between coordination and progress on the assigned task.

Anthropic (blog post of 10 August 2026, an unreleased model) used a coordinator in Claude Code and about 60 subagents with written briefs in the second session. The proven share of simple zeros of the zeta function on the critical line rose from 5/12 to 2/3. The blog says "41.6% to 67.2%". Discovering cryptographic weaknesses with Claude

Anthropic; ETH Zurich; Tel Aviv University; TU Berlin · Jul 2026

Anthropic's Frontier Red Team weakened HAWK and 7-round AES-128, each result costing about $100,000 in API usage; the number of workers and a single-agent control are not disclosed.

More
Authors
Anthropic (Frontier Red Team)
Organizations
Anthropic; ETH Zurich; Tel Aviv University; TU Berlin
Published
28 Jul 2026
Status
Company blog and technical reports, not peer-reviewed
Source
anthropic.com/research/discovering-cryptographic-weaknesses

Anthropic's Frontier Red Team (blog post of 28 July 2026, Claude Mythos Preview) attacked the post-quantum signature scheme HAWK and sped up an attack on AES-128. Per the blog, the cost of HAWK-256 key recovery fell from 264 to 238, and HAWK-512 from 2150 to 2108. The attack on 7 of 10 rounds of AES-128 sped up from 299 to 289.3–291.4. Each result cost about $100,000 in API usage. HAWK took about 60 hours in a "multi-agent" setup supervised by one researcher. AES took about a billion output tokens and three substantive human hints. The only multi-agent episode described: the key HAWK idea was found by a pair of workers who argued until they agreed. HAWK's designers withdrew the scheme from the NIST process on 29 July 2026. The number of workers, the protocol and a single-agent control are not disclosed. Failed sessions are not included in the cost.

AI Agent Swarms as Researchers: Progress, Challenges, and Open Questions

Purdue University · Sep 2026

"Thousands" of Claude Code and Codex subagents produced 45 paper candidates totalling 1,699 pages in a few weeks; this is volume and a review queue, not measured quality.

More
Authors
Sergey Gusev, David E. Bernal Neira
Organizations
Purdue University
Published
28 Sep 2026
Status
arXiv preprint
Source
arxiv.org/abs/2609.35719

Sergey Gusev and David Bernal Neira (Purdue University; preprint of 28 September 2026) gave Claude Code and Codex, mostly Claude Fable 5.1 and GPT-6 Astra, a topic and an instruction to make correct progress and not to stop. They contributed no scientific ideas of their own. Over a few weeks, "thousands" of uncounted subagents produced 45 paper candidates (22 full drafts) and 8 lab programmes not yet carried out, 1,699 pages in total. 5 results are marked as fully proved in Lean and 13 as partially proved. The subscriptions cost $800 a month. At API prices the same logs would have cost more than $15,000 over four weeks. Checking the 53 items will, by the authors' estimate, take more than seven months. No major errors were found in the part read so far. This is volume and a review queue, not quality, and there is no control.

Reinforcement learning on multi-agent traces

Swarms trained with reinforcement learning are already in products: the Kimi K2.5 "conductor" from Moonshot AI (K2.6 also has a swarm, but how it was trained is not disclosed) and GPT-5.6, which according to OpenAI was trained "end-to-end" with native multi-agent orchestration (card above). Academic work shows the same on small models: Tsinghua on a 4-billion-parameter model, a group led by the Chinese University of Hong Kong on an 8-billion-parameter orchestrator for tasks with images, audio and video. The published GPT-5.6 curves already allow a comparison of single-agent and swarm modes at equal total output tokens. They do not isolate the contribution of multi-agent training from system architecture and context management. Where the single agent is given comparable conditions, the gain shrinks to a few points.

Kimi K2.5: Visual Agentic Intelligence — Agent Swarm and PARL (+ Kimi K2.6 swarm update)

Moonshot AI · Jan 2026

Kimi K2.5: a swarm "conductor" trained with reinforcement learning reaches a target WideSearch level 3.0–4.5 times faster than a single agent; against a single agent with context cleanup, the BrowseComp gain is +3.5 pp.

×
Fig. 4.9original ↗
More about this work
Authors
Kimi Team, Tongtong Bai, Yifan Bai, Yiping Bao, S. H. Cai et al. (336 authors)
Organizations
Moonshot AI
Published
27 Jan 2026
Status
Technical report (arXiv preprint)
Source
arxiv.org/abs/2602.02276

Moonshot AI (Kimi Team; K2.5 technical report on arXiv 2 February 2026, model and blog post 27 January) described the Agent Swarm mode. One copy of the model acts as a "conductor" (orchestrator): it decides how many helpers to create, what prompts to write for them and which subtasks to hand out in parallel. The helpers do not talk to each other and each returns a single message. The novelty is PARL: the conductor was trained with reinforcement learning on swarm rollouts with "frozen" helpers. To keep the model from sliding back into working alone ("serial collapse") and from spawning empty helpers for reward ("spurious parallelism"), bonuses for parallelism and for the share of helpers that finish were temporarily added to the reward. Time was counted in "critical steps".

  • According to the report, on WideSearch the swarm reaches a target Item-F1 (30–70%) 3.0–4.5 times faster than a single agent. Over training, accuracy rises from about 37% to 64%, and the average number of concurrent helpers from ~8 to ~14.
  • For K2.5, BrowseComp is 78.4% versus 60.6% for a single agent (+17.8 pp; GPT-5.2 Pro 77.9%), WideSearch 79.0 versus 72.7, an internal benchmark 58.3 versus 41.6. But 60.6% is a single agent without context management, for which context overflow counts as failure. With simple context cleanup it scores 74.9%, and the swarm's gain shrinks to +3.5 pp.
  • For K2.6 (20 April 2026) the swarm gives 86.3% on BrowseComp versus 83.2% for a single agent with context cleanup. A single K2.6 agent on WideSearch (80.8) already beats the K2.5 swarm (79.0). With a small step budget, the swarm is worse than a single agent.

A trained swarm is in a released product, and the training method is described. RL on multi-agent traces buys speed plus a few points on divisible search. The swarm's tokens and costs are not disclosed, so "a trained swarm beats one long-running agent at equal cost" has not been shown.

This is a self-report without replication or ablations, and the contribution of RL and of each bonus is not isolated. The speedup is shown on one benchmark in nominal "critical steps". The swarm was tested only on search tasks and a closed internal benchmark built for parallelization. The limits of up to 100 helpers and 1,500 steps (K2.5) and of 300 and 4,000 (K2.6) are claims from blog posts, not measurements, and there are no safety evaluations of the swarm.

benchmark / dataseteconomics & scaleplatforms & simulatorsself-organizationSwarm Bench does not report the number of agents, tasks or seeds

WideSeek-R1: Exploring Width Scaling for Broad Information Seeking via Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning

Tsinghua University; Infinigence AI · Feb 2026

Multi-agent RL on Qwen3-4B lifts item F1 on WideSearch to 40.0% versus 28.1% for a single agent trained on the same data; the curve keeps rising up to 10 subagents.

More
Authors
Zelai Xu, Zhexuan Xu, Ruize Zhang, Chunyang Zhu, Shi Yu et al. (10 authors)
Organizations
Tsinghua University; Infinigence AI
Published
4 Feb 2026
Status
arXiv preprint (under review)
Source
arxiv.org/abs/2602.04634

Tsinghua University and Infinigence AI (Xu and co-authors; arXiv v3 of 12 March 2026, v2 withdrawn for lack of approval from all authors) trained a single Qwen3-4B to act both as the lead and as parallel subagents (up to 10) on about 20 thousand "build a table" tasks. On WideSearch, item F1 is 40.0% versus 28.1% for a single agent trained on the same data and 31.2% for an untrained multi-agent Qwen3-4B; the published result for a single DeepSeek-R1-671B is 41.3% (on whole rows, 20.7 versus 15.3). For the trained system the curve rises up to 10 subagents, while for the untrained one it starts to fall at ten subagents; extra turns for a single agent quickly plateau. A narrow but real example of RL on multi-agent traces moving quality; the hierarchy is fixed, recursion is off, and the system produces a fully correct table on 0.4% of tasks.

benchmark / dataseteconomics & scaleplatforms & simulatorstraining (RL)Number of simultaneously active subagents not disclosed; 2 in parallel in an example

Orchestra-o1: Omnimodal Agent Orchestration

CUHK; LIGHTSPEED; Peking University; Tsinghua University; Tongji University · Jun 2026

Orchestra-o1 with GPT-5 as the lead agent scores 72.8% on OmniGAIA versus 53.9% for a single agent on the same model; for the 8B orchestrator, most of the gain comes from the scaffold, not training.

More
Authors
Fan Zhang, Vireo Zhang, Shengju Qian, Haoxuan Li, Hao Wu, Jinyang Wu et al. (11 authors)
Organizations
CUHK; LIGHTSPEED; Peking University; Tsinghua University; Tongji University
Published
10 Jun 2026
Status
arXiv preprint
Source
arxiv.org/abs/2606.13707

The Chinese University of Hong Kong, Peking University, Tsinghua, Tongji and LIGHTSPEED (preprint of 10 June 2026) built Orchestra-o1: a lead agent splits a question involving images, audio or video into subtasks, picks a model and tools for each, and runs the independent ones in parallel. On OmniGAIA (360 tasks), the variant with GPT-5 as lead scored 72.8% versus 62.5% for Gemini-3-Pro with tools (+10.3 pp), and a single agent on the same GPT-5 with the same tools 53.9%. An 8B orchestrator (Qwen3-8B), trained with offline reinforcement learning on individual decisions, scored 30.0% versus 20.8% for the best previous open model, but most of this comes from the scaffold itself (26.3% without training), and the helpers in the published settings are GPT-5. One run, the Gemini rows are taken from the OmniGAIA table, and tokens and cost versus a single agent were not compared.

Theory: how much parallel labour can deliver

Ord's λ estimates (0.48–0.68) match the assumptions of "intelligence explosion" models: the AI Futures Model uses λ = 0.5, Davidson and Houlden 0.6. Ord notes that he got just the values these models assumed, although he had "hoped that the value of λ for AI agents would be lower". In these models λ determines whether AI researchers working in parallel can accelerate progress up to a vertical asymptote. Two 2026 works approach the question from different sides: Trammell shows that without growth in "parallelization technology" an explosion is not guaranteed, while Bradshaw bets that organized communication will make returns superlinear.

theory / essayeconomics & scaletheoryTheoretical work: no runs or agents

The Bounded Parallelizability of R&D: Theory and Application to AI

Epoch AI; Stanford Digital Economy Lab · Jun 2026

Trammell adds a second ceiling to growth models — parallelization technology; how fast it grows determines whether an "intelligence explosion" happens and how fast it is. Theory without data.

More
Authors
Philip Trammell
Organizations
Epoch AI; Stanford Digital Economy Lab
Published
13 Jun 2026
Status
Working paper and Epoch AI essay, not peer-reviewed
Source
philiptrammell.com/static/Parallelizability.pdf

Philip Trammell (Epoch AI and the Stanford Digital Economy Lab; working paper of 13 June 2026, revised version 24 July, Epoch essay 29 July) adds a second ceiling to growth models, parallelization technology: how much work can be split up, done simultaneously and put back together. If this ceiling grows no more slowly than the stock of researchers, the usual "intelligence explosion" conclusion holds; if more slowly but faster than the technological frontier, the explosion still happens, only more slowly; if it merely sustains an exponential, automation can raise the growth rate but does not accelerate it without bound. There are no data of his own: the author writes that the parameters have barely been estimated and proposes an experiment that is not in the paper — give ever larger groups of agents a task like the NanoGPT speedrun and see when extra agents start duplicating work. A reason not to expect unbounded acceleration from the number of agents alone, not a measurement; the paper says nothing about the quality of a swarm's output.

theory / essayeconomics & scaletheoryNo runs of its own; agent counts cited from others' work

Swarm Organization as the Exponent on Test-Time Compute

Not stated · Sep 2026

Bradshaw forecasts (65% within a year, 85% within two) that organized communication will make returns to parallel compute superlinear; he admits this has not been measured.

More
Authors
Julian Bradshaw
Organizations
Not stated
Published
17 Sep 2026
Status
LessWrong post
Source
lesswrong.com/posts/EnpJ29asosMKcFGL8/swarm-organization-as-the-exponent-on-te…

Julian Bradshaw (LessWrong, 17 September 2026; no affiliation given) forecasts that organized communication will make returns to parallel compute superlinear (exponent β > 1 in E ≈ C·Nβ) "within some meaningful range of swarm sizes": 65% within a year, 85% within two. He admits that this has not been measured, that Brown considers today's speedup slightly sublinear, and that at Anthropic the Mythos swarm (266 findings) and independent agents (21; Chapter 7), compared per token on the same parts of the code, came out "roughly just the same". The "something like $22 million" for Navier–Stokes is Zvi Mowshowitz's estimate for an outside customer (internal marginal costs "several million"), and the price drop of 40× per year is a single Epoch example from March 2025 (range 9–900×), not a law. A forecast, not data.

Takeaways

  • Lab data: at equal total tokens a single GPT-5.6 Sol agent is no worse than a swarm (λ = 0.48–0.68, Ord); an Opus 5.5 team reaches the same level faster but spends more tokens (Opus 5.5 card). In these measurements the swarm's main benefit is time.
  • Under a shared token budget the team's effect depends on the task and architecture, not on agent count alone: the 0.0% mean in Kim et al. is heterogeneity (95% CI −58.7…+77.2%), not absence of an effect.
  • Quality gains above the single-agent ceiling come with conditions; the one reported in a lab system card came at a price (Opus 5: +3.1 pp on BrowseComp at ≈2.5× cost); a new model generation gives more than a tenfold larger team (Opus 5.5, Lean). λ gives a rough advance estimate of the cost (see the box).
  • Communication raises the final result where intermediate progress is verifiable and agents share findings (Park et al., DeLM, CORAL). Debate loses to voting over independent answers (Wisconsin), and automatically built systems usually do not beat voting (Salesforce).
  • Open: the published GPT-5.6 curves already compare single-agent and swarm modes at equal total output tokens, but do not isolate multi-agent training from system architecture and context management; Kimi K2.5 still has no equal-token comparison of its trained swarm with one long-running agent; high-profile campaigns (Navier–Stokes, zeta function, HAWK) run without a single-agent control; swarms of thousands lack an equal-token single-agent comparison.

    Tom Davidson, Tom Houlden (Forethought), 4 Aug 2025

Chapter 5 of 11

Self-organization: structure without a boss

Can identical agents with a shared channel divide work themselves, and does that beat a designed organization? Structure emerges. In several comparisons reviewed here, peer teams underperformed a coordinator, a prescribed scheme, or a single agent, or offered no gain. Positive results also exist, including DeLM (Ch. 6) and the Opus 5.5 team discussed below; organizations whose rules an outer loop works out in advance also win.

Give identical agents a shared channel, a mailbox, a board or git, and no assigned roles. Can they split the work, and is the organization that appears better than a scheme drawn in advance, or better than one agent at an equal budget? Equal-budget comparisons here mean matched tokens or compute, not matched wall-clock time. A structure showing up in the logs, and that structure beating an alternative, are different results. The chapter keeps them apart: first what equal agents actually do when the logs are open, then what companies found after a flat swarm failed on their own code, then controlled comparisons, then rules an outer loop selects before the task starts. The summary table records what each study equalized. Physical swarms at the end are a contrast in method. One word, "self-organization", covers three mechanisms in these papers, and they do not support the same conclusion.

Three different kinds of "self-organization"

The works in this chapter use one word for three different things, and the conclusions differ.

  1. Emergence on the fly. Equal agents with a shared channel divide the work themselves, during the task: Agensh, Carlini's compiler, AutoScientists, AI Village (Chapter 10). This is self-organization in the pure sense.
  2. Learned organization. An outer loop works out the rules of collaboration before the work starts, and they are then frozen: Self-Organizing Agent Teams (a debrief after each task), Sakana's Conductor and TRINITY (reinforcement learning and evolutionary search). No human draws the scheme, but equal agents do not invent it from scratch either: at Sakana a trained dispatcher picks the workers and roles for each task, and in Self-Organizing Agent Teams the frozen strategies set the order of the conversation, while the division of work on a new task forms and changes as the work proceeds.
  3. Designed through iteration. Engineers read failure logs and redraw the scheme: Cursor, Anthropic Labs, Cognition. The authors sometimes call the result "emergent", but that is their interpretation, not an experiment.

Equal agents with a shared channel: what the logs show

This section collects runs in which many identical agents get a shared channel, a task and time, while humans stay only in the outer loop. Two such runs with open data appeared in 2026: a thousand Microsoft Research agents on one program and sixteen Anthropic agents on a compiler; a third observation, without open logs, comes from the Claude Opus 5.5 system card. All three show that structure emerges; none compares it with a designed one.

experiment with LLM agentseconomics & scaleself-organizationplatforms & simulators1,024 participants; peak 1,020 active in a 37.5-second interval

Agensh: Scaling Organizational Intelligence to 1,024 Agents

Microsoft Research · Sep 2026

Up to 1,024 identical agents with no dispatcher hold together and put forward roles on their own; the pandoc pass rate rises from 33.89% (one agent) to 55.06%. Time (6 h) was equalized, not compute.

×
Fig. 5.1original ↗
×
Fig. 5.2original ↗
More about this work
Authors
Zhihao Zhan, Ting Song, Li Dong, Shaohan Huang, Jianxun Lian et al. (7 authors)
Organizations
Microsoft Research
Published
22 Sep 2026
Status
arXiv preprint (technical report)
Source
arxiv.org/abs/2609.26781

Zhihao Zhan, Ting Song, Li Dong and colleagues at Microsoft Research, in "Agensh: Scaling Organizational Intelligence to 1,024 Agents", handed one large programming task to an organization of identical agents and no dispatcher. Every worker is the same model, GPT-5.6-sol, in the Copilot harness, with the same prompt except for the worker ID. They share a Gitea Git server, a Mattermost chat and a board for claims, findings and failures. The task is from ProgramBench: in 6 hours, with no Internet, rebuild an existing program (FFmpeg, gromacs, pandoc, PHP-src or ctags) given only the ability to run it. Teams of 1, 8, 32 and 128 agents ran on all five tasks, and 1,024 agents on pandoc only. The prompt still prescribes a detailed work cycle, so "no structure" means "no boss".

The mean share of hidden tests passed rises with team size, but not evenly.

  • Mean over the tasks: 19.31% with one agent, 20.68% with 8, 26.52% with 32 and 28.78% with 128 (+9.47 pp).
  • Pandoc: 33.89 → 41.75 → 43.11 → 50.94 → 55.06% for 1, 8, 32, 128 and 1,024 agents, about +2 pp per doubling (our estimate). On pandoc, 128 agents exceeded 30% by minute 30; the single agent did not get there in two hours.
  • The curves jump. On ctags, 8 agents do worse than one (18.47% vs 25.82%). On PHP-src, 128 agents fell from 28.47% in the second hour to 12.35% at the finish, and the paper does not explain the drop.

Pandoc and ctags supply almost all of the gain.

Cooperation in the logs grows with team size: agreements on interfaces at 8 agents, then reviewers chosen by experience, a negotiated integration protocol, and self-nominated "integrators" at 128 and 1,024. A replay of the 1,024-agent run (our count) shows 11,111 pull requests, 2,800 merged (25%), and 148,079 board entries, 56,472 of them FAIL. Identical prompts and a shared channel of messages, board and git are enough for roles to appear among a thousand participants. Nothing in the study compares that with a designed hierarchy. The authors' claim that an orchestrator would be the bottleneck is an argument, not a measurement. Time was matched and compute was not: tokens were not counted, the 128- and 1,024-agent teams received roughly 117 and 800 times the agent-hours of one agent (our estimate), and the single agent was still improving at hour six. It stayed on the task for all 6 hours only because of a "stop hook". No repeats are reported. This is a preprint, and the code is unavailable (404).

engineering reporteconomics & scaleself-organizationplatforms & simulators16 agents in parallel; one project of about 2 weeks

Building a C compiler with a team of parallel Claudes

Anthropic · Feb 2026

In two weeks, 16 Claude Opus 4.6 copies with no boss or messaging wrote a C compiler that builds a bootable Linux kernel; parallelism broke where the task was indivisible. One run, no control.

×
Fig. 5.3original ↗
More about this work
Authors
Nicholas Carlini
Organizations
Anthropic
Published
5 Feb 2026
Status
Lab engineering blog
Source
anthropic.com/engineering/building-c-compiler

Nicholas Carlini's "Building a C compiler with a team of parallel Claudes" is a two-week run of 16 copies of Claude Opus 4.6. Each copy sat in its own container with a clone of one shared git repository and the job of writing, in Rust, a C compiler that builds the Linux kernel. There was no boss, and the agents did not message each other. An agent claimed a task with a lock file in git; a double claim made the push fail, and the second agent had to pick something else. Notes and a queue of ideas also lived in files. Every session started with an empty context.

By Carlini's account the run used nearly 2,000 sessions, 2 billion input tokens and 140 million output tokens, for just under $20,000. The compiler he describes as "100,000-line" builds a bootable Linux 6.9 kernel for x86, ARM and RISC-V, compiles QEMU, SQLite, PostgreSQL and Doom, and passes 99% of the tests in most suites. The repository history, counted here, shows 3,982 commits from 23 January to 5 February 2026, 2,005 claims, 14 team restarts and 186.7 thousand lines of Rust. While the kernel build was one indivisible task, all 16 agents hit the same bug, wrote the same fix and overwrote each other. The split that unblocked them was a human scheme: a reference GCC compiles some files and the new compiler the rest, so the work becomes independent pieces.

The open git history is what makes the run readable. Agents choose tasks and carry a real project to a result, and the same history shows where self-organization stops. A human assigned the five specialist roles (deduplication, speed, efficiency of the generated code, architecture critic, documentation), designed the tests, the CI and the reference scheme, and restarted the team about 14 times. There is no single-agent control at the same budget and no other team size. The prompt, the tests and the harness are unpublished. The compiler is also weaker than the headline. Carlini says GCC still compiles the 16-bit x86 boot code, the compiler's own assembler and linker are rough, and the code it generates is slower than GCC with optimizations disabled. Late changes broke earlier ones, which he treats as a limit of the model. John Regehr found 34 miscompilation bugs. SPEC CPU2017 programs showed on average more than 70% lower performance than when built with GCC, and some of the bugs match bugs in the hobby compiler chibicc.

The third observation is in the Claude Opus 5.5 system card (Anthropic, 22 September 2026; the economics of these teams are in Chapter 4). A hundred agents build a hierarchy themselves, and the task sets its shape. Teams of 1, 10, 30 and 100 agents worked for 24 hours on one machine on two tasks: a knowledge base about a company, and a theorem in Lean. The harness designated only the lead. Who messaged whom, and who handed work to whom, the agents decided. On the Lean task the 100-agent team formed two tiers. The lead stayed in contact with 12 "sub-leads". Each sub-lead handed work to its own group of 3–9 helpers and reported back, and five more helpers reported to the lead directly. "Sub-lead" is the authors' post-hoc definition: a helper from whom at least three other helpers received more messages than from any other agent. On the knowledge base the same hundred stayed flat. The lead split the records in advance, and the helpers barely talked. The structure was read from the transcripts of one 100-agent team per task. The card does not measure any gain of the two-tier scheme over the flat one, and team size, as Chapter 4 shows, adds almost nothing to the result beyond 10 agents.

Fig. 5.4original ↗

AutoScientists: Self-Organizing Agent Teams for Long-Running Scientific Experimentation

Harvard University · May 2026

A team with no central planner beats a single-agent loop, but at 4.4× the money.

More
Authors
Shanghua Gao, Ada Fang, Marinka Zitnik
Organizations
Harvard University
Published
27 May 2026
Status
arXiv preprint
Source
arxiv.org/abs/2605.28655

Shanghua Gao, Ada Fang and Marinka Zitnik (Harvard), in "AutoScientists: Self-Organizing Agent Teams for Long-Running Scientific Experimentation", run long scientific searches on Claude Sonnet 4.6. The default team is 6 experimenters and 3 analysts. Teams discuss directions, vote, and reorganize when a direction stalls. On 24 BioML-Bench tasks the mean leaderboard percentile is 74.40 against 66.07 for the single-agent Autoresearch loop. Starting from an already strong champion on training a small GPT, the team accepted 7 improvements out of 93 attempts and the single agent 0 out of 100. Self-organization is limited: roles are set at launch, and after a vote one agent, the alphabetically last analyst, records who is on which team. Tokens cost about 4.4 times as much ($3,985 vs $910). A 14-agent team is worse than a smaller one on every task of the size check. The report is a preprint.

The Dot and the Swarm

One Useful Thing (Substack) · Oct 2026

A popular-essay version of the idea that agents no longer need to be assigned roles in advance; in his Navier–Stokes example, according to OpenAI's page, people steered the swarm's work.

More
Authors
Ethan Mollick
Organizations
One Useful Thing (Substack)
Published
1 Oct 2026
Status
Blog (Substack)
Source
oneusefulthing.org/p/the-dot-and-the-swarm

Ethan Mollick's essay "The Dot and the Swarm" (1 October 2026) argues that a swarm divides the work by itself. His example is the Navier–Stokes effort (the chronicle is in Chapter 2, the economics in Chapter 4). OpenAI's page says the result came from a group of "on the order of 10,000 concurrent agents" in about 88 hours, and that the whole effort took 2.7 million messages and about 130 billion output tokens. Mollick writes "thousands" of agents. The same OpenAI page describes the human role: people chose the problem, moved agents onto it from other problems, prompted them with the Euler result and cross-pollinated groups through Codex. That is thinner than an org chart and thicker than "no one organized them". The Clay prize has not been awarded. This is a blog, not a measurement.

Engineering experience: flat swarm vs hierarchy

Companies that run swarms on real codebases reached similar conclusions independently: a swarm of equals did not work for them, and a scheme drawn by people did. Cursor described the path in three posts. It started with a swarm of equals and redrew the scheme from failure logs.

engineering reporteconomics & scaleplatforms & simulatorsself-organizationPeak ≈2,000 according to an interview; 1,173 cumulative on a single file in another run

Cursor: series of posts on agent swarms — “Scaling long-running autonomous coding”, “Towards self-driving codebases”, “Agent swarms and the new model economics”

Cursor (Anysphere) · Jan 2026

The flat "swarm of equals" failed: 20 agents worked at the throughput of one to three. A hierarchy of planners and workers, found by engineers from the logs, worked. Company posts, no control.

×
Fig. 5.5original ↗
×
Fig. 5.6original ↗
More about this work
Authors
Wilson Lin
Organizations
Cursor (Anysphere)
Published
14 Jan 2026
Status
Company blog (series of three posts)
Source
cursor.com/blog/scaling-agents

Wilson Lin's three Cursor posts (14 January, 5 February and 20 July 2026), "Scaling long-running autonomous coding", "Towards self-driving codebases" and "Agent swarms and the new model economics", describe hundreds of coding agents spending weeks on one project without humans: a browser in Rust from scratch, then SQLite in Rust from the documentation alone. The first version was a swarm of equals, same status and a shared coordination file with locks, then optimistic concurrency control and a "wait for another agent" tool.

The flat scheme failed quickly. Twenty agents worked at the throughput of two or three (first post) or one to three (second). Agents held locks too long, forgot to release them, or used them incorrectly. Dropping locks cut the overhead, and without a hierarchy the agents became risk-averse, made small safe changes, and nobody took the hard end-to-end tasks. What worked, across four design generations read off the logs, was a root planner, recursive sub-planners with narrow areas, and workers on their own copies of the repository. Each worker does one task without coordinating and hands in a written report. The central integrator was removed as a bottleneck.

The browser run peaked at about 1,000 commits per hour and went a week without intervention. The public repository has 29,858 commits and 1.74 million lines of Rust in the main src folder. On SQLite in 4 hours, the new swarm passed 73–85% of hidden tests against 11–77% for the old one. With Grok 4.5 the old swarm ran away: more than 70,000 merge conflicts, one file edited by 1,173 agents, stopped before the two-hour mark. The new one had fewer than 1,000 conflicts. The flat swarm was tried on a real project with three coordination mechanisms, and it did not work. People designed the hierarchy that did. "Emergent" in the second post is the author's reading of that redesign, not an experiment that let the organization appear. Chapter 4 covers what the runs cost. These are company posts about Cursor's own system: no paper, no harness code, no logs, no comparison with one agent at the same budget, and no count of attempts per configuration. Lin asks readers to look at trends. The flat scheme was tried only briefly, on the models of winter 2025/26, and with three mechanisms. The browser did not build at publication; a fixed version appeared 2–3 days later.

engineering reportself-organizationplatforms & simulatorseconomics & scaleSimultaneous count not stated; example of up to 10 sessions

Cognition: “Don't Build Multi-Agents” (2025) and “Multi-Agents: What's Actually Working” (2026)

Cognition AI · Jun 2025

Cognition's experience: parallel writing by several agents does not work; a narrow class of schemes does, in which agents contribute intelligence but one agent writes. Vendor opinion without tables.

More
Authors
Walden Yan
Organizations
Cognition AI
Published
12 Jun 2025
Status
Two company blog posts
Source
cognition.com/blog/dont-build-multi-agents

Walden Yan (Cognition, makers of Devin) argued in "Don't Build Multi-Agents" (June 2025) against multi-agent systems: parallel writers make implicit decisions that conflict, and one linear agent with the full context is more reliable. In "Multi-Agents: What's Actually Working" (April 2026) he says that still holds for parallel writing, and he names the schemes that do work. The Devin Review reviewer, starting from a clean context, finds on average 2 bugs per PR, about 58% of them severe, and pairing Claude and GPT helps on the hardest cases. He calls unstructured swarms mostly a distraction, names "map-reduce-and-manage" as the practical shape, and notes that messaging between agents does not emerge by itself, because the models were not trained for it. The numbers come with no sample size and no baseline.

engineering reportself-organizationplatforms & simulatorseconomics & scale1 active agent; 3 roles run sequentially with file handoffs

Harness design for long-running application development

Anthropic · Mar 2026

On one prompt, a three-agent harness with assigned roles built a playable game maker where solo Opus 4.5 did not, but took 6 hours and $200 vs 20 minutes and $9, from an expanded spec.

More
Authors
Prithvi Rajasekaran
Organizations
Anthropic
Published
24 Mar 2026
Status
Lab blog (engineering blog)
Source
anthropic.com/engineering/harness-design-long-running-apps

Prithvi Rajasekaran (Anthropic Labs, 24 March 2026), in "Harness design for long-running application development", built three agents with assigned roles: a planner, a generator, and an evaluator that clicks through the app with Playwright. The only direct comparison he describes in detail is one prompt for a retro game maker. Solo Claude Opus 4.5 worked 20 minutes for $9. The harness worked 6 hours for $200. In the solo version the entities did not respond to input. In the harness version one could control a character and play, although Rajasekaran got stuck at one wall. The task was not the same: the planner expanded the one-sentence prompt into 16 features. His lesson is about the harness, not about the score. Every component is an assumption about what the model cannot yet do alone, and it comes out as soon as a newer model manages without it. With Opus 4.6 the sprints disappeared. The planner and the evaluator stayed, evaluation moved to the end of the run, and, he says, the evaluator pays off on tasks beyond what the model does reliably solo. One run, no benchmark.

The Anthropic campaign in which agents searched protein databases for new enzyme systems (23 September 2026; Chapter 6 goes through the memory) is organized from the top as well. The announcement's "roughly 950 agents" are 949 Claude Mythos 5 sessions over 21.5 hours, with at most 58 able to run at once. Worker, supervisor, curator of the shared knowledge base, and editor were roles set in advance. The five stages ran as a chain: the next opened only when a script accepted the previous one. Initiative also came from below. The agents opened 98 of the 119 tasks themselves. Neither a flat team nor a single agent was tried on the same task, so the campaign is a working practice, not a comparison.

Controlled comparisons: equal agents vs a coordinator, a designed scheme and a single agent

The company stories have no control arm. The controlled studies that do, mostly from 2026, usually find the same thing when the alternative is a coordinator, a prescribed scheme, or one agent doing the same work: the peer team loses or ties. The exceptions later in the section are specific. An external turn order with self-chosen roles can beat a central coordinator. Five peers on ProgramBench can beat a lead-and-subagents scheme on speed and tokens, with the same final quality. Those are not a general win for an unstructured swarm.

experiment with LLM agentsbeliefs & consensusecho & diversityself-organizationTeams of 2, 4 and 8 agents

Multi-Agent Teams Hold Experts Back

Stanford University; Apple; Emory University · Feb 2026

With no expert named, a team without roles does not beat its best member on any of five benchmarks; on ranking tasks non-experts average the expert's view with theirs, and the gap grows with size.

×
Fig. 5.7original ↗
×
Fig. 5.8original ↗
More about this work
Authors
Aneesh Pappu, Batu El, Hancheng Cao, Carmelo di Nolfo, Yanchao Sun et al. (7 authors)
Organizations
Stanford University; Apple; Emory University
Published
1 Feb 2026
Status
arXiv preprint; accepted at ICML 2026
Source
arxiv.org/abs/2602.01011

Aneesh Pappu, Batu El and colleagues, in "Multi-Agent Teams Hold Experts Back" (ICML 2026), tested a pure swarm of equals. Several different models, with no roles and no rules, discuss a task for four rounds and give a joint answer. The teams were built on purpose from one strong model and several weak ones, for example GPT-5 with GPT-4o and Claude 3.5 Haiku. The suite is five AI benchmarks and three psychology ranking tasks in which one or several members hold the knowledge.

When the expert is not named, the team does not beat its best model on any benchmark. It does beat the average member and simple voting.

  • Team vs best model: 86.0% vs 86.5% (MMLU-Pro), 76 vs 78 (GPQA), 51 vs 52 (SimpleQA), 28 vs 29 (HLE), 63 vs 73.5 (MATH-500).
  • If the team could pick, on every question, the answer of whoever was right, it would score 91.8 / 88.8 / 62.3 / 47.5 / 79.0%. The shortfall from that ceiling is 6.3% to 41.1% relative.
  • Naming the expert, a hint that does not exist in practice, lets the team beat the best model on four benchmarks of five, and it still misses the ceiling.

On the psychology tasks the team's error is 17–113% higher than the expert's. Members do not defer even to a named expert.

  • Non-experts treat the expert's view as something to average with their own, an "integrative compromise" (r = 0.55 and 0.69 with the loss, significant on two tasks of three), and experts give in.
  • Larger teams (2 → 4 → 8) have a larger gap (r = 0.32–0.59). Four experts together do about as well as one, so the cause is unequal knowledge, not conversational noise.
  • One saboteur barely hurts (26.0 vs 26.7).

The hope under test is that equal agents will organize themselves around the strongest member. They average that member out, which is why the authors recommend explicit roles. The next paper by the same first and senior authors, below, is about learned organization rather than this free discussion. The mechanism was measured on cheap 2024 models (Claude 3.5 Haiku, GPT-4o-mini). GPT-5, Opus 4.5 and Sonnet 4.5 appear only in the benchmark part, 100 questions each and no confidence intervals, so gaps of 0.5–2 pp may be chance. The format is one question, four rounds, no tools and no memory. Long-running swarms were not tested. The team makes 25–28 model calls per task instead of one (our estimate from the protocol), and tokens are not reported. The explanation through training for agreeableness is the authors' hypothesis.

benchmark / datasetbeliefs & consensusecho & diversityplatforms & simulatorsUp to 4 agents in a small experiment on 46 tasks; the main benchmark uses pairs

CooperBench: Why Coding Agents Cannot be Your Teammates Yet

Stanford University; SAP Labs US · Jan 2026

The "curse of coordination": a pair of agents loses, on average across five models, 41% of single-agent capability.

More
Authors
Arpandeep Khatua, Hao Zhu, Peter Tran, Arya Prabhudesai, Frederic Sadrieh et al. (11 authors)
Organizations
Stanford University; SAP Labs US
Published
19 Jan 2026
Status
arXiv preprint
Source
arxiv.org/abs/2601.13295

Arpandeep Khatua, Hao Zhu and colleagues at Stanford and SAP Labs, in "CooperBench: Why Coding Agents Cannot be Your Teammates Yet", built 652 tasks. Two agents receive different features that overlap in the code. Their changes are merged, and the tests for both features run. GPT-5 succeeds as a pair 0.28 of the time against 0.48 when one agent gets both features. Claude Sonnet 4.5 scores 0.26 vs 0.47, MiniMax-M2 0.14 vs 0.36. Chat reduces merge conflicts and does not raise success. On 46 tasks, growing the team from 2 to 3 and 4 agents drops success from 68.6% to 46.5% and 30.0%. Role division appears on its own, rarely. Of 50 analysed failures, 42% are a wrong picture of the partner, 32% broken commitments and 26% failed exchanges. The study is a preprint about pairs: small teams rather than large swarms.

Worse Together: How Performance Breaks Down in Multi-User Multi-Agent Teams

Independent; Stanford University; Anthropic · Sep 2026

A team with one agent per user is worse than a single coordinator in every environment, and a mailbox does not close the gap.

More
Authors
Sahan Paliskara, Nattaput Namchittai, Andrew Lampinen
Organizations
Independent; Stanford University; Anthropic
Published
30 Sep 2026
Status
arXiv preprint
Source
arxiv.org/abs/2610.00583

Sahan Paliskara, Nattaput Namchittai and Andrew Lampinen, in "Worse Together: How Performance Breaks Down in Multi-User Multi-Agent Teams" (preprint, 30 September 2026), describe 77 scenarios in four environments. Each user has an agent, and the resource is shared: an API budget, a merge queue, a clinic, a personal assistant placing a joint order. On the API budget, peer-to-peer reaches 30% of the optimum with Claude Opus 5 and 12% with Sonnet 5, against 64% and 32% for a coordinator. Without a communication channel those peer shares are 7% and 2%. As the team grows from 4 to 16 agents, the share of Sonnet 5 agents that act falls from 66% to 10%. In the clinic a silent Opus 5 team beats a communicating one (81.0% vs 69.5%). The gap closes with an appointed lead, a strict instruction, or a block on the order until the other agents' messages have been read. That is an assigned role, not a hierarchy the agents built. The benchmark has not been released yet.

When Agents Coordinate: Measuring Coordination in Multi-Agent AI Coding

University College London · Aug 2026

The task, not the agents, sets the shape of the communication network, and the word "coordinator" in the prompt changes nothing.

More
Authors
Giuseppe Destefanis, Tomaso Aste
Organizations
University College London
Published
17 Aug 2026
Status
arXiv preprint
Source
arxiv.org/abs/2608.16801

Giuseppe Destefanis and Tomaso Aste, in "When Agents Coordinate: Measuring Coordination in Multi-Agent AI Coding", recorded 1,902 runs of teams of 1 to 16 Claude Sonnet 4.6 agents in Claude Code on two synthetic Python tasks, plus 244 runs in a sealed environment. The agents do build a network. A shared specification produces a dense network (mean degree 5.47 at 8 agents), a pipeline a sparse one (2.99), and at 16 agents direct links nearly vanish (0.28). The phrase "you are the coordinator" creates neither a hub nor a gain. In the sealed replication a flat team and a team with a "coordinator" are not significantly different (20/30 vs 21/32, p = 1.00). An eight-step chain passes in 9 of 10 runs with two and four agents and in 0 of 10 with eight, once a rounding rule falls on the boundary between two owners. One model, and large variance between sessions.

Organizational Principles Enable Collective Intelligence in Embodied AI

Duke University · Sep 2026

A hierarchy drawn by a human beats both a scheme drawn by a model and dynamic leadership.

More
Authors
Zhengran Ji, Jonathan Hyun, Boyuan Chen
Organizations
Duke University
Published
10 Sep 2026
Status
arXiv preprint
Source
arxiv.org/abs/2609.11737

Zhengran Ji, Jonathan Hyun and Boyuan Chen at Duke, in "Organizational Principles Enable Collective Intelligence in Embodied AI", ran teams of up to 50 heterogeneous agents in the CREW-Wildfire firefighting simulator: 25 missions, eight models, five seeds. The ORCH scheme is drawn before the start and is not changed during the mission. The abstract reports +63.97% in score and +74.29% in efficiency for the human scheme relative to four earlier multi-agent algorithms, and +43.63% and +52.53% for the model-drawn scheme. Raw means are not in the text. Episode success is 53.1% for the human scheme, 42.2% for the model with a critic, 35.2% without a critic, and 6.9–13.5% for the baseline systems, including CAMON with its "emerging leadership" (1,000 runs each). It is a simulation, with no single-agent comparison, and a language model did the error analysis.
