Drop the Hierarchy and Roles: How Self-Organizing LLM Agents Outperform Designed Structures

Moscow Institute of Physics and Technology (MIPT) · Mar 2026

An external turn order with self-selected roles beats both a central coordinator and fully simultaneous work.

More
Authors
Victoria Dochkina
Organizations
Moscow Institute of Physics and Technology (MIPT)
Published
30 Mar 2026
Status
arXiv preprint
Source
arxiv.org/abs/2603.28990

Victoria Dochkina's "Drop the Hierarchy and Roles: How Self-Organizing LLM Agents Outperform Designed Structures" compares coordination protocols for groups of 4 to 256 agents on synthetic tasks. Another model judges quality. The winning protocol, Sequential, keeps an external turn order. Agents choose only their role. On GPT-4.1-mini with 8 agents the scores are 0.724 for Sequential, 0.640 for a central coordinator and 0.503 for fully simultaneous Shared (+44%, Cohen's d 1.86). On Claude Sonnet 4.6, DeepSeek v3.2 and GLM-5, with 16 agents and GPT-5.4 as judge, Sequential beats the coordinator by 14.1%, 12.4% and 12.2%. Shared was not re-run. From 64 to 256 agents quality does not change (about 0.96). Agents invent role names almost every time (5,006 unique names), and at 256 agents about 45% withdraw on their own. One author, and the data are not released. The 25,000 runs stated in the abstract do not match the table (about 20,810).

A counterexample in favour of equals is in the Claude Opus 5.5 system card, §8.12.1–8.12.2, with more of the economics in Chapter 4. Five peer agents, each holding the full task, the same tools and a messaging channel, were compared with a scheme in which a lead spawns subagents and those subagents see only the lead's instructions. On ProgramBench, measured on "an alternate, broadly comparable snapshot" of the model, the peer five reached 60% of hidden tests faster, about 38 vs 66 minutes of derived latency, and spent fewer tokens to get there, about 1.1 vs 2 million. Both pairs of numbers are our estimates from the charts. The final ceiling is the same for both schemes and for the single agent. On DRACO, under a tight time budget, the lead spawns subagents much less often and prefers to work alone, while a pre-assembled team keeps dividing the work. The gain is speed and tokens, not final quality. People designed both schemes. In this comparison the flat team of equals did not lose to the hierarchy.

Learned organization: an outer loop selects the rules

If equal agents do not find a good structure during the task, and a scheme drawn by people goes stale, a third option is to let a separate loop select the rules in advance, by reinforcement learning or by a debrief after each task. This is not a swarm of equals. No human draws the scheme, and the agents do not invent it as they work. Only the division of labour adapts to the specific task. That is the second kind in the box at the start of the chapter.

Self-Organizing Agent Teams Learn to Reason Together

Stanford University; Together AI; Emory University · Sep 2026

The learned organization of three different models beats both a single model and hand-designed protocols (66.7% vs 58.7% for one model at roughly the same budget), but on some benchmarks the gain almost vanishes.

×
Fig. 5.9original ↗
×
Fig. 5.10original ↗
More about this work
Authors
Aneesh Pappu, Mirac Suzgun, Yongchan Kwon, Federico Bianchi, Batu El et al. (8 authors)
Organizations
Stanford University; Together AI; Emory University
Published
19 Sep 2026
Status
arXiv preprint
Source
arxiv.org/abs/2609.22682

Aneesh Pappu and James Zou, the same first and senior authors as "Multi-Agent Teams Hold Experts Back", with second author Mirac Suzgun and colleagues at Stanford, Together AI and Emory, tried to teach a team from its own experience. The paper is "Self-Organizing Agent Teams Learn to Reason Together". Three different models, o3-mini, Claude Sonnet 4 and DeepSeek-V3, solve 15 AIME 2024 problems. After each problem the strongest member runs a debrief and rewrites the strategy: who speaks in which phase, who checks, who argues with the majority, how to reach a conclusion. A bank of 10 strategies was frozen and applied to new problems. A second team, Gemini-2.5-Flash, Llama-4-Maverick and GPT-4.1, learned the same way on knowledge tasks.

On five mathematics and physics benchmarks the team solves 66.7% of problems on average.

  • The best model alone gets 48.8%.
  • The same model at roughly the same compute budget, called linearization because one model serially plays every role, gets 58.7%.
  • A routing oracle that always picks the best of the members' answers gets 59.0%.
  • Mixture of Agents gets 57.3%.
  • Three copies of o3-mini with the same strategies get 56.0%.

On benchmarks newer than the models the picture splits.

  • AIME 2026, released after all three models, gives the team 71.2%: 29.0 pp above the best model and 13.4 pp above the routing oracle, but only 2.7 pp above linearization (68.5%).
  • HMMT 2026 is a tie at 39.4%.
  • On knowledge tasks the gain almost vanishes, 72.8% vs 72.1% for linearization, and the team sits below the routing oracle (79.6%) even though a correct candidate exists for 87.9% of problems.

Generation outruns selection. The easier it is to tell correct reasoning from incorrect, the larger the gain: ρ = 0.90 across 8 benchmarks (p = 0.005).

The organization is learned and still flexible. The authors say the strategies do not prescribe the subtasks of a new problem. The division of reasoning forms, is contested and changes during the discussion. The team beats hand-designed protocols, and nobody specified the roles it invented, a "consensus challenger" and a "final auditor". The design that remains is real. The three-model roster is fixed in advance, the strongest member picks the final answer, and the conversation rules were learned on examples with known answers rather than found during the new task. On some benchmarks the team barely beats one model at the same budget. Model heterogeneity is a noticeable part of the gain (Chapter 8). The code is not released. The models are from the 2025 generation, chosen to "preserve headroom", and the effect is untested on 2026 flagships. The team always has three members. Tokens, cost and time are not reported. The linearization budget is matched only "approximately", and the team makes on the order of 80–110 model calls per problem (our estimate).

experiment with LLM agentseconomics & scaleself-organizationtraining (RL)Sequential orchestrator and up to 5 steps; number of active agents not stated

Sakana AI: learned orchestration of several LLMs — Conductor, TRINITY, Fugu

Sakana AI; University of Michigan; Institute of Science Tokyo · Dec 2025

Sakana's small trained "conductors" choose which LLM handles a task and in which role; Conductor scores 77.27 vs 74.78 for the best single model (GPT-5).

More
Authors
Yujin Tang, Edoardo Cetin, Jinglue Xu, Qi Sun, Stefan Nielsen et al. (14 authors)
Organizations
Sakana AI; University of Michigan; Institute of Science Tokyo
Published
4 Dec 2025
Status
ICLR 2026 (Conductor, TRINITY); Fugu report, blog: not peer-reviewed
Source
arxiv.org/abs/2606.21228

Sakana AI trained small conductor models that choose which of other providers' LLMs receives a task, and in which role. The line of work is Conductor and TRINITY (both ICLR 2026) plus the Fugu technical report (June 2026). Conductor, a 7B model trained with reinforcement learning, writes a plan of at most 5 steps and averages 77.27 across 7 benchmarks against 74.78 for the best single model (GPT-5). In a controlled setting the scores are 72.35 against 67.60 for a single model with five times the context. TRINITY, a 0.6B coordinator with the roles Thinker, Worker and Verifier, scores 70.44 against 61.12–65.95 when the same protocol runs on a single model. Fugu-Ultra, on top of Gemini 3.1 Pro, Opus 4.8 and GPT-5.5, scores 73.7 on SWE-Bench Pro against 69.2 for Opus 4.8. No human draws the topology, and equal agents do not invent it either. A central orchestrator chooses, within at most five steps. The competitor numbers for Fugu are taken from vendors and leaderboards.

Agentic AI and the next intelligence explosion

Google; University of Chicago; Santa Fe Institute; Antikythera; UC San Diego · Mar 2026

A conceptual article in Science: the "intelligence explosion" will be a society of agents that self-organizes at every level, but within institutions designed by people. No data.

More
Authors
James Evans, Benjamin Bratton, Blaise Agüera y Arcas
Organizations
Google; University of Chicago; Santa Fe Institute; Antikythera; UC San Diego
Published
19 Mar 2026
Status
Science (peer-reviewed journal), short opinion piece
Source
doi.org/10.1126/science.aeg1895

James Evans, Benjamin Bratton and Blaise Agüera y Arcas (Google, University of Chicago, Santa Fe Institute, Berggruen Institute) argue in "Agentic AI and the next intelligence explosion" (Science, 19 March 2026) that the intelligence explosion will be a society of agents, not one giant mind. An agent facing a hard task spawns specialized copies, and those spawn their own sub-societies: self-organization at every level. They also call for designing institutions, role protocols and constructive conflict as deliberate features, devil's advocacy and brainstorming among them, and for replacing dyadic alignment through RLHF with institutional alignment, humans in the loop. The anchor is the authors' own preprint on societies of thought inside reasoning models, plus an analogy with past evolutionary transitions. The article is an argument. It reports no measurement.

Summary: what was compared with what

The studies disagree largely because they compare different things and equalize different things. The table is the control conditions, not a ranking.

Work Agents What vs what Equalized on Outcome
Agensh 1–1,024 identical swarm of equals vs single agent time (6 h), not tokens structure emerges; quality rises with N: 128 agents give +9.47 pp across five tasks at ~117× the agent-hours; ~800× applies only to the 1,024-agent pandoc run (agent-hours are upper bounds, our estimate)
C compiler 16 identical no control — structure emerges; roles assigned by a human
Opus 5.5 card, §8.12.3 1–100 identical team sizes against each other; structure without a control time (24 h) structure emerges (two tiers on Lean, flat on the knowledge base); beyond 10 agents the returns to size are small
Cursor: two posts from the three-post series hundreds flat swarm (20 agents) vs hierarchy — "Towards self-driving codebases"; old hierarchy vs new — "Agent swarms and the new model economics" flat: not equalized; versions: time (4 h) the flat swarm failed: 20 agents worked at the throughput of 1–3; the hierarchy was found by people
Teams Hold Experts Back 2–8 different team without roles vs best member number of questions, not calls the team does not beat the best member
CooperBench 2–4 pair vs single agent with the same work amount of work the pair is worse than the single agent
Worse Together 4–52 agent per user vs one coordinator scenario the team of equals is on average worse than the coordinator (roughly half as good on the API budget and in the assistant)
ORCH up to 50 human scheme vs model-drawn scheme and dynamic leadership missions and seeds the human scheme is better
Self-Organizing Agent Teams 3 different learned adaptive organization vs single model at ~the same budget ~compute +8.0 pp on average, 0–2.7 pp on some benchmarks

⟷ scroll the table sideways

Takeaways

  • Structure emerges in a swarm of equals: interface agreements and self-nominated "integrators" in Agensh, 2,005 task "claims" in git for the C compiler, two tiers with 12 "sub-leads" among 100 Opus 5.5 agents; also AI Village and OpenAI's agent organization, which ended in an incident.
  • In several controlled comparisons, peer teams underperformed alternatives or offered no gain: a team without roles does not beat its best member, a pair loses 41% of solo capability, one agent per user loses to a coordinator, a human scheme beats "emerging leadership".
  • Without a human architect, organization wins at comparable cost when an outer loop (training or debriefs) sets its rules in advance: Self-Organizing Agent Teams, 66.7% vs 58.7% for one model at roughly the same budget; see also Conductor.
  • Equal agents divide work where the task splits into independent pieces with a checkable success criterion (the C compiler kernel build was split by file), and fail where work needs trust in others' reports, end-to-end ownership and deference to an expert.
  • Open: no one has tested a large swarm of equals against a designed team on one task at equal token budget. The closest, the Opus 5.5 card, uses only five agents: peers beat a lead with subagents on speed and tokens, with equal final quality.

Related work

  • ToolOrchestra: Elevating Intelligence via Efficient Model and Tool Orchestration
    NVIDIA; The University of Hong Kong; November 2025, ICML 2026 (poster)

    An 8B model trained with RL to direct other models and tools scores 37.1% on HLE vs 35.1% for GPT-5 while being 2.5 times more efficient

  • Multi-Agent Collaboration via Evolving Orchestration
    Tsinghua, SJTU, Siemens, Tencent Robotics X, BUPT; May 2025, NeurIPS 2025

    A central "puppeteer" trained with RL chooses on the fly which agent acts next; during training the structure becomes more compact and more cyclic

  • Multi-agent Architecture Search via Agentic Supernet
    NUS, Tongji, NTU, Shanghai AI Lab, USTC; February 2025, ICML 2025

    Instead of a fixed team, a "supernet" of architectures from which a separate one is chosen for each query: 6–45% of the inference cost, accuracy higher by 0.54–11.82%

  • Heterogeneous Swarms: Jointly Optimizing Model Roles and Weights for Multi-LLM Systems
    University of Washington; Google Cloud AI Research; February 2025, NeurIPS 2025

    A system of different LLMs as a graph optimized by a swarm algorithm: on average 18.5% better than 15 baselines on 12 tasks; the gain comes from model diversity

  • AgentNet: Decentralized Evolutionary Coordination for LLM-based Multi-Agent Systems
    SJTU; SII; April 2025, NeurIPS 2025

    Agents without a central orchestrator rewire the graph themselves and refine their specialization through retrieval-augmented memory; the authors claim higher accuracy than a single agent

  • OrgAgent: Organize Your Multi-Agent System like a Company
    CUHK; CISPA; IBM Research; April 2026, preprint

    A governance–execution–compliance hierarchy usually beats flat schemes and uses fewer tokens; +102.73% is one cell (GPT-OSS-120B, SQuAD 2.0), not an average

  • Loop-Back Authority in LLM Agent Teams
    Leiden University (LIACS); September 2026, preprint

    Five identical agents, 43 task pairs: when a manager can send work back for revision, reports get worse (d = 0.42 in favour of the flat scheme) and use 51.5% more tokens

  • Effective Strategies for Asynchronous Software Engineering Agents
    Carnegie Mellon; March 2026, preprint

    A manager builds a plan with dependencies and agents work in parallel in isolated git copies: +25.6 pp on PaperBench and +14.7 on Commit0 vs one agent (authors)

  • MAS-Orchestra: Understanding and Improving Multi-Agent Reasoning
    Salesforce Research; MIT; UW–Madison; January 2026, ICML 2026

    An orchestrator trained with RL assembles the whole system in one go; a controlled benchmark shows that the benefit depends on the structure of the task

  • Small Model as Master Orchestrator (ParaManager)
    SJTU; Shanghai AI Lab; CUHK; Sichuan University; Fudan University; April 2026, preprint

    A small model learns to break a task into parallel subtasks for agents and tools and outperforms static pipelines

  • Intelligent AI Delegation
    Google DeepMind; February 2026, preprint

    A conceptual framework for delegation between agents and humans: assigning tasks together with authority, responsibility and trust; no experiments

  • SwarmBench: Can Large Language Models Act as Agent Swarm Orchestrators?
    CASIA; UCAS; August 2026, EMNLP 2026 Findings (per the arXiv comment)

    8 tasks and 400 examples on how models split work, spawn subagents and aggregate results; aggregation is most often the hardest skill

  • Reinforcement Learning for LLM-based Multi-Agent Systems through Orchestration Traces
    independent author; May 2026, survey preprint

    84 papers across 8 reward families and 5 orchestration decisions; as of May 2026 no explicit RL method for deciding "when to stop" was found

  • Controlling Collectives of AI Agents in Reasoning Space with Spatial Transformers
    University of Pennsylvania (GRASP); September 2026, preprint

    Flocks of up to 1,024 simulated robots driven by a frozen Qwen3-1.7B fly on natural-language commands; heading error falls from 2.79° at N = 8 to 0.34° at N = 1,024

  • Unraveling the emergence of collective behavior in networks of cognitive agents
    University of Padua (Zomer, De Domenico); March 2026, npj Artificial Intelligence

    A small study in a Nature Portfolio journal: 20 agents running one 2023 7B model on two-dimensional functions with spurious minima are more reliable than a single agent but slower than a classic particle swarm; in networks with fast information exchange they get stuck at a false optimum more often (the links were set by the experimenters)

For comparison: physical swarms

The same collective dynamics, phase transitions, majority-following, order without a centre, reappear in physical bodies that have neither language nor prompts. Three 2026 papers in Nature Physics, Science Advances and Nature Communications demonstrated them on tens to thousands of devices. For agent swarms they are a reference, not an analogy to stretch: the count of bodies is exact, and Zakir et al. measured one effect from a mathematical model through to hardware.

Phase coherence and disorder-induced wave propagation in micromotor arrays ENS de Lyon, CNRS, LPENSL (Braun, Poncet, Bartolo); Leiden University (Morin); Nature Physics, 11 August 2026; preprint arXiv:2604.07080, April 2026 a 48 × 44 = 2,112 lattice of Quincke motors thousands of 3D-printed rotating motors, given neither a pattern nor a direction, settle by themselves into a clean antiferromagnetic phase with phase coherence of their precession; introduced disorder triggers free propagation of phase waves across the lattice
Fluidic torque–enabled object manipulation by microrobot collectives University of Michigan; Max Planck Institute for Intelligent Systems, Stuttgart; Cornell University; Koç University (Ceron, Gardi, Petersen, Sitti); Science Advances, 27 February 2026 a dense collective of 1,000 magnetic disks of Ø 300 µm and 20 passive objects of Ø 1 mm a collective of microrobots manipulates foreign objects through hydrodynamic (fluidic) torque
Bio-inspired decision making in robot swarms under biases IRIDIA, Université Libre de Bruxelles (Zakir, Dorigo); naXys, University of Namur (Carletti); University of Konstanz and Max Planck Institute of Animal Behavior (Reina); Nature Communications, 14 August 2026 50 Kilobot robots in 24 experiments; 100 robots in the ARGoS simulator; a Gillespie model at N = 50, 100, 200 and 400 under "asocial" interference (stubborn robots, self-sourced information, corrupted messages) a cross-inhibition rule borrowed from living swarms yields fast, consistent and robust collective choice, while direct switching gets stuck in deadlock; tested at three levels: physical robots, simulator, mathematical model

⟷ scroll the table sideways

Chapter 6 of 11

Swarm memory

What does memory beyond a single model's window give a swarm? External memory helps a single agent too; shared team notes raise answer accuracy with diminishing returns, and facts distributed across agents do not add up without an exchange protocol.

Swarm memory is not reducible to the sum of context windows. Agents store and pass on knowledge through files, claim graphs, repositories and the shared environment. Three results need to be kept apart:

  • Working beyond the window. External memory helps a single agent too; by itself this is not a swarm advantage.
  • Joint accumulation of knowledge. In Anthropic's experiment with Opus 5.5, answer accuracy from the team's notes rises from 0.53 for one agent to 0.70 for ten and 0.74 for a hundred (Ch. 4). This is answer accuracy, not a measurement of memory capacity.
  • Pooling distributed facts. Knowledge spread across members does not guarantee a correct shared answer.

The chapter moves from the limits of the native window and a single agent's external memory to shared swarm artefacts, the pooling of distributed facts, and population and environment memory.

The native window grew; reasoning over it did not

A long window covers storage and retrieval, but not reasoning over the whole window. The Claude Opus 4.6 measurements below separate those two jobs. Quality falls with length even inside the window: in 2025 Chroma described "context rot" across 18 models (the longer the input, the less reliable the answer, and unevenly across positions), and LOCA-bench (HKUST, February 2026) showed an agent's accuracy dropping fast as its context grows from 8 to 256 thousand tokens with the task held fixed, with a clear benefit from context management (both works are in the table at the end of the chapter). This is the niche the swarm lays claim to: not fitting more in, but not losing the thread.

system cardmemoryeconomics & scaleplatforms & simulatorsOrchestrator and subagents; number of subagents not disclosed

Claude Opus 4.6: launch post (1M-token context), system card (multi-agent BrowseComp) and Claude Code agent teams

Anthropic · Feb 2026

Claude Opus 4.6's window grew to 1 million tokens: retrieving eight "needles" scores 76.0% (MRCR v2), but a multi-step graph task of the same size reaches only F1 38.7–41.2 (GraphWalks).

More
Authors
Anthropic
Organizations
Anthropic
Published
5 Feb 2026
Status
Launch post, system card and documentation
Source
anthropic.com/news/claude-opus-4-6

On 5 February 2026 Anthropic released Claude Opus 4.6 with a 1 million-token window (beta): on MRCR v2 with eight "needles" in about a million tokens of text the model scores 76.0% at maximum effort with adaptive thinking, against 18.5% for Sonnet 4.5 with a 64 thousand-token thinking budget; the settings differ. But on a multi-step graph task of the same size (GraphWalks BFS) its F1 is only 38.7–41.2. Multi-agent search in the system card (an orchestrator with no tools of its own and subagents with windows of 200 thousand tokens each) reaches 86.57% on BrowseComp against 83.73% for the best single agent, and 92.5% against 91.3% F1 on DeepSearchQA; the number of subagents and the token counts are not disclosed. "Agent teams" in Claude Code remain an experimental feature with a permanent lead, a shared task list and mailboxes. Storage is solved; reasoning over the whole window is not.

Memory through many calls: a single agent beyond the window

Memory beyond the window does not require a swarm. The text is moved into the environment (a variable, a file, a directory), and the model reads it in chunks, calls itself on fragments, or edits its own context as a document; this way a single agent already works with corpora of millions and trillions of tokens. These works show that "effective memory" exceeds the window by orders of magnitude, and they set the bar for a swarm: if one agent with a file system already handles a corpus of trillions of tokens, swarm memory has to deliver something beyond that.

experiment with LLM agentsmemoryeconomics & scaleplatforms & simulatorsMain experiments: 1 call at a time; LongCoT-mini parallelism not stated

Recursive Language Models

MIT CSAIL · Dec 2025

The model keeps text in a variable and calls itself on chunks of it: over a corpus of 6–11 million tokens (22–40 times GPT-5's window) it answers correctly 91.3% of the time; the sub-calls do not communicate with each other.

×
Fig. 6.1original ↗
×
Fig. 6.2original ↗
More about this work
Authors
Alex L. Zhang, Tim Kraska, Omar Khattab
Organizations
MIT CSAIL
Published
31 Dec 2025
Status
arXiv preprint
Source
arxiv.org/abs/2512.24601

Alex Zhang, Tim Kraska and Omar Khattab (MIT CSAIL), in "Recursive Language Models" (preprint, 31 December 2025), put the long text in a variable inside an environment where the model runs Python. The root model sees only the length and the beginning of the text, writes code that searches and filters it, and from that code calls a language model, itself or a cheaper one, on individual chunks. Sub-calls return strings, know nothing about each other, and in the main experiments ran one after another. The memory is the variable plus the code that reads it, not a conversation among copies of the model.

On BrowseComp-Plus with 1,000 documents (6–11 million tokens, 22–40 times GPT-5's window of 272 thousand), RLM(GPT-5) answers correctly 91.3% of the time. GPT-5 itself cannot take the input.

  • An agent with BM25 retrieval scores 51.0%, and a compaction agent 70.5%.
  • On OOLONG-Pairs, where the answer depends on almost every pair of lines, F1 rises from 0.1 for GPT-5 to 58.0 at depth 1 and 76.0 at depth 3.
  • Quality still falls with length, only more slowly: on OOLONG the RLM drops from 84.5% at 8 thousand tokens to 46.0% at 1 million, and GPT-5 from 92.1% to 31.1% already at 262 thousand.
  • The median gain across four tasks is +26% over the compaction agent, +130% over CodeAct with sub-calls, and +13% over Claude Code (on Claude Opus 4.1). Against the OpenCode coding agent on the same GPT-5 the margin is about 2%.

Cost per query is on average comparable to the base model, with a heavy tail.

Those baselines sit on the same GPT-5, so the gain is not a stronger model in disguise. It is still not a swarm. Sub-calls do not communicate, there is no speed-up (RLMs take longer), and much of the effect comes from moving the text into a programmable environment. The test sets are small (20–150 examples), the runs are single and without intervals, and the tasks are mostly synthetic. On short inputs the plain model is better. Deep recursion helps GPT-5 on the hardest task but mostly hurts Qwen3-Coder, and an independent reproduction (CUHK) found that depth 2 lowers accuracy. NeurIPS 2026 acceptance is according to the author's blog.

benchmark / datasetmemoryplatforms & simulatorseconomics & scaleOne coding agent per example; no team

Coding Agents are Effective Long-Context Processors

Duke University · Mar 2026

A single coding agent with a directory of text and ordinary commands beats the best published numbers on four of five long-context benchmarks (samples of 200 examples); a search index more often hurts it.

More
Authors
Weili Cao, Xunjian Yin, Bhuwan Dhingra, Shuyan Zhou
Organizations
Duke University
Published
20 Mar 2026
Status
arXiv preprint
Source
arxiv.org/abs/2603.20432

Weili Cao, Xunjian Yin, Bhuwan Dhingra and Shuyan Zhou (Duke), in "Coding Agents are Effective Long-Context Processors" (preprint, 20 March 2026), give a single agent, Codex on GPT-5 or Claude Code, a directory with the text and ordinary commands: file search and Python. On samples of 200 examples, best configuration vs best published number: BrowseComp-Plus 88.50 vs 80.00, Oolong-Synthetic 71.75 vs 64.38, Oolong-Real 37.46 vs 24.09, Natural Questions (a corpus of about 3 trillion tokens) 56.00 vs 50.90; on LongBench-v2, 62.50 vs 63.30. The average of +17.3% is a mean of relative gaps, and Oolong-Real contributes more than half of it. A search index more often hurts: Codex without one scores 88.50 on BrowseComp-Plus, with BM25 78.50; the coding agent also beats the recursive RLM. An argument for a single agent's file-based memory, not for a swarm; some of the "best published" numbers were obtained on the full sets.

Context Language Models

University of Washington; Meta Superintelligence Labs; MIT; Trillium Labs · Sep 2026

The model edits its own context as a file: an agent on Qwen3.6-27B with a 32 thousand-token window scores 59.4% on BrowseComp-Plus; the only swarm experiment, with 6 agents, was run once.

More
Authors
Rulin Shao, Shannon Zejiang Shen, Junjie Oscar Yin, Yuetai Li, Minheng Wang et al. (13 authors)
Organizations
University of Washington; Meta Superintelligence Labs; MIT; Trillium Labs
Published
29 Sep 2026
Status
arXiv preprint
Source
arxiv.org/abs/2609.37725

Rulin Shao and 12 co-authors at the University of Washington and Meta Superintelligence Labs, in "Context Language Models" (preprint, 29 September 2026), keep the context in a file that the model edits itself through bash, instead of a transcript compressed by an external harness. Without fine-tuning, an agent on Qwen3.6-27B with a 32 thousand-token window scored 59.4% on BrowseComp-Plus (11.4% higher than the best summarization baseline, at lower compute). The only swarm experiment: 6 GPT-5.6-Sol agents, one per repository, optimize interdependent Python packages for more than a day; the speed-up on held-out tests is 1.044× vs 1.026× for a swarm with summarization at the same spend. Subagents barely help: 44.2 vs 44.6 with a 32 thousand-token window, and only at 128 thousand 50.2 vs 47.3, at about +54% compute. The preprint is dated 29 September 2026; the swarm was run once.

experiment with LLM agentsmemorytraining (RL)economics & scaleTree of copies of one policy: depth up to 12 at evaluation

Recursive Agent Optimization

Carnegie Mellon University; Amazon AGI Labs · May 2026

A model trained to spawn copies of itself with a clean window solves 95% of synthetic tasks with an 8 thousand-token window vs 24% for a single agent; on DeepDive recursion is about 18 times slower.

More
Authors
Apurva Gandhi, Satyaki Chakraborty, Xiangjun Wang, Aviral Kumar, Graham Neubig
Organizations
Carnegie Mellon University; Amazon AGI Labs
Published
7 May 2026
Status
arXiv preprint
Source
arxiv.org/abs/2605.06639

Apurva Gandhi, Aviral Kumar and Graham Neubig (CMU), with Satyaki Chakraborty and Xiangjun Wang (Amazon AGI Labs), in "Recursive Agent Optimization" (preprint, 7 May 2026), train a model to spawn copies of itself with a clean window. The copy starts with an empty window, so the parent does not have to keep the child's scratch work. On synthetic crafting, a recursive agent with an 8 thousand-token window solves 95% of tasks and 88% of hard ones, almost as well as with a 256 thousand-token window, while a single agent at 8 thousand stays at 24% and 0%. When the single agent's window is large enough, it still solves only 20% of hard tasks against 88%, and on hard tasks solved by both, recursion is 2.5 times faster. On Oolong-Real the reward rises from 0.203 to 0.320 and on DeepDive success from 0.24 to 0.40, but there, on tasks solved by both, recursion is about 18 times slower and spends 561 thousand tokens vs 20 thousand. The prompt encourages delegation; the models are Qwen with 4 and 30 billion parameters.

Six more works of the same family are collected, with their numbers, in the table at the end of the chapter: MemAgent (the agent reads text in chunks and rewrites a short memory), Context-Folding from ByteDance Seed (folding a subtask into a brief summary), Recursive Agent Harnesses from PwC and Prime Agent from Prime Intellect, Princeton and MIT (recursive subagents), Cursor's Composer 2 (training together with chains of self-summaries), and Anthropic's engineering blog, which in September 2025 reduced practice to three techniques: context compaction, structured notes outside the window, and subagents with a clean context that return a brief summary.

Shared artefacts in real swarms

When there are many agents working for weeks, memory is no longer the window but what lies between agents: a claim graph, a repository, a shared folder, an index of notes. In 2026 such memory held several swarms together, and each case shows its cost. An example of accumulation is the knowledge base that up to 100 Opus 5.5 agents build in a day (Ch. 4): nearly all of the gain in answer accuracy on it comes by 10 agents, and going from 10 to 100 adds only 4 pp. Cursor, whose swarm wrote SQLite, explains its success by context efficiency: "We suspect the ability to scale the agent swarm comes from this context efficiency, more than from parallelism itself." A planner never implements and never clutters its window with details, a worker never plans, and a shared Field Guide is curated by the agents themselves; this is the author's hypothesis, without an ablation. Kimi K2.5 calls its swarm "proactive context management": each helper works in its own window and returns a single result. As early as 2025, Anthropic engineers wrote that the lead agent of their Research system stores its plan in external memory because context beyond 200 thousand tokens is truncated (Ch. 4). Below are Anthropic's enzyme-discovery campaign, where a dedicated agent maintains the shared knowledge base, and four more works that make such external memory an object of study.

engineering reportself-organizationplatforms & simulatorseconomics & scale949 sessions in the main campaign; limit of 58 simultaneous, actual peak not stated

Autonomous AI agents discover reverse transcriptases with tandem repeat arrays

Anthropic · Sep 2026

A campaign of Claude Mythos 5 agents with a shared knowledge base found a new branch of enzymes in 21.5 hours, but the decisive detail was seen by one worker that read the raw DNA; 10 reruns missed it.

×
Fig. 6.3original ↗
More about this work
Authors
Peter H. Yoon, Januka S. Athukoralage, Emmanuel Ameisen, Eric Kauderer-Abrams, Nicholas T. Perry et al. (6 authors)
Organizations
Anthropic
Published
23 Sep 2026
Status
Lab technical report and announcement (not peer-reviewed)
Source
anthropic.com/news/claude-discovers-novel-enzyme-system

Anthropic, in the report "Autonomous AI agents discover reverse transcriptases with tandem repeat arrays" (23 September 2026; Peter H. Yoon, Januka S. Athukoralage, Emmanuel Ameisen and colleagues), tasked its agent system with finding new reverse transcriptase systems, enzymes that copy RNA into DNA, in a metagenomic protein database. Every role was played by Claude Code with Claude Mythos 5. Humans set the structure: five stages of the brief with scripted checks, worker–supervisor pairs, and a report editor. Memory was a shared version history plus a knowledge base that a "curator" agent updated after each task; relevant entries from it were injected into the prompts of later agents.

In 21.5 hours without human intervention the run produced 119 tasks (98 of them opened by the agents themselves), 949 sessions and 215.6 million tokens, excluding cache reads. The announcement's "roughly 950 agents" is the sum of short sessions in different roles: the system allowed at most 58 at once, and on average 3–4 were running (our estimate). The find was a new branch of ART enzymes with an array of CRISPR-like repeats. The decisive step was taken by one worker that read raw DNA in its own window: neither the brief nor the task assignment asked about repeats. In 10 reruns of the same campaign the array was never noticed. In a test of single models on the already found data, the strongest models found the array in at least 90% of attempts when the DNA was placed directly in the prompt, while working through files and tools the share fell to as low as 32% (Opus 5).

The shared base carried leads between sessions, and the detail was still seen only by the agent that read the raw data itself. The hierarchy was set from above (Chapter 5), and there is no "single agent" or "team without a lead" control (Chapter 4): this is an application example, not a scaling measurement. This is a company report without peer review: the brief, the code and the skill library are unpublished, the lab experiments were done by humans, and, by the authors' own account, the enzyme's activity and the system's function have not been shown. Mario Rodriguez Mestre (University of Copenhagen) says his group studied these enzymes for years and discussed unpublished data with Claude; Anthropic replies that it knows of no publications on the system and did not train on users' conversations.

engineering reportself-organizationeconomics & scaleplatforms & simulators“Dozens” of agents worked in parallel; exact maximum not given

Formalizing Fermat's Last Theorem

Anthropic; Columbia University; Imperial College London · Sep 2026

"Dozens" of Claude agents wrote a Lean 4 proof of Fermat's Last Theorem (13 million lines) in 11 days once they switched to the shared Prove2Me claim graph; there is no single-agent control.

More
Authors
Anthropic
Organizations
Anthropic; Columbia University; Imperial College London
Published
4 Sep 2026
Status
Lab blog; not peer-reviewed
Source
anthropic.com/research/formalizing-fermats-last-theorem

Anthropic (blog, 4 September 2026; the project was led by Tianyi Peng, whose group at Columbia University builds Prove2Me): "dozens" of Claude agents, from 7 to 17 August 2026 (11 days), wrote the first complete machine-checked proof of Fermat's Last Theorem in Lean 4: 29,511 theorems, 13 million lines, about 6 billion output tokens; checked by the Lean kernel and by the independent nanoda kernel. The first attempts failed: agents "quickly lost track of the project's state and stopped collaborating effectively" (their work still accounts for about 7% of the final non-boilerplate lines). What held the later work together was Prove2Me, a shared dependency graph. Each node is a statement plus a description in ordinary language; an agent reads that graph, checks another agent's statement, and only then tries to prove it. The authors call the graph "particularly helpful for mitigating memory degradation". There is no single-agent control, the effects of model and platform are confounded, and the number of simultaneous agents and the cost are not disclosed; the proof relies on Mathlib.

engineering reportmemoryself-organizationtraining (RL)13 worker accounts; plus 3 manual agent sessions; setup account excluded

Agora: Git as Shared Memory for Collective AutoResearch

NVIDIA · Sep 2026

Shared agent memory as a Git commit graph: 13 workers took a frozen model from 3.3923 to 1.899 bits per byte, but about 98% of the reduction came in the first six hours.

More
Authors
Yifan Zhang, Yunheng Zou, Shaokun Zhang, Jian Hu, Hao Zhang et al. (8 authors)
Organizations
NVIDIA
Published
16 Sep 2026
Status
arXiv preprint
Source
arxiv.org/abs/2609.18094

Eight NVIDIA authors, among them Yifan Zhang and Yi Dong, in "Agora: Git as Shared Memory for Collective AutoResearch" (preprint, 16 September 2026), built shared agent memory as a Git commit graph: every result, failure and check references its parents, and there are no roles. From 26 April to 8 May 2026, 13 workers (eight of them on Claude Opus 4.7, GPT-5.5 and GPT-5.4; the models of the other five were not recorded) took a frozen model from 3.3923 to 1.899 bits per byte (62% of the way to a trained GPT-2 124M), leaving 1,703 contributions, including 165 checks; the winner's lineage is 145 commits from 15 accounts. Almost all of the gain came in the first six hours: the first 18 scored contributions closed about 98% of the reduction, after which the search narrowed into a single branch, and on 2 May humans changed the prompt of eight workers. The gain per unit of compute was not measured: v1 proposed four equal settings, and v4 removed that table.

CORAL: Towards Autonomous Multi-Agent Evolution for Open-Ended Discovery

MIT; NUS; MiniMax; McGill; Stanford; SambaNova; SMART · Apr 2026

Long-running agents with a shared memory of attempts and notes: four agents without roles reached 1,103 cycles on Anthropic's kernel task vs 1,350 for one agent, at the cost of 596 evaluations vs 56.

More
Authors
Ao Qu, Han Zheng, Zijian Zhou, Yihao Yan, Yihong Tang et al. (17 authors)
Organizations
MIT; NUS; MiniMax; McGill; Stanford; SambaNova; SMART
Published
2 Apr 2026
Status
arXiv preprint; published at COLM 2026
Source
arxiv.org/abs/2604.01658

Ao Qu, Han Zheng, Zijian Zhou and colleagues at MIT, NUS and SMART, in "CORAL: Towards Autonomous Multi-Agent Evolution for Open-Ended Discovery" (COLM 2026, preprint April 2026), replace fixed evolutionary search with long-running agents that share a memory of attempts and notes. A later agent can start from a commit another agent already made. A single agent on Claude Opus 4.6 is not worse than OpenEvolve, ShinkaEvolve and EvoX on any of 11 tasks (strictly better on 9) and is strictly above the previous record on 6. Four agents without roles on Anthropic's kernel task reach 1,103 cycles vs the previous record of 1,363 and 1,350 for one agent; the best of four independent runs stays at 1,180; 66% of the new records grew out of another agent's commit. Without accumulated notes and skills, a single agent degrades from 1,350 to 1,601 cycles. The price: 596 evaluations vs 56, and the share of successful ones falls from 43% to 9%; the claimed number of new records does not match the table.

Decentralized Multi-Agent Systems with Shared Context

Stanford University · Jun 2026

Peer agents without a lead write findings into a shared context: on SWE-bench Verified, at the same cost of $0.24 per task, two DeLM agents score 63.72% vs 55.26% for AOrchestra (Gemini 3 Flash).

More
Authors
Yuzhen Mao, Jerry Gu, Aadi Chauhan, Qizheng Zhang, Hangoo Kang et al. (6 authors)
Organizations
Stanford University
Published
9 Jun 2026
Status
arXiv preprint
Source
arxiv.org/abs/2606.10662

Yuzhen Mao, Azalia Mirhoseini and colleagues at Stanford, in "Decentralized Multi-Agent Systems with Shared Context" (preprint, 9 June 2026), remove the lead agent. Peer agents take tasks from a queue and write findings into a shared context, so the next agent inherits the finding without inheriting the search that produced it. On SWE-bench Verified with Gemini 3 Flash, two-agent DeLM and AOrchestra cost the same, $0.24 per task, while accuracy is 63.72% vs 55.26%; four agents give 66.08% at $0.47. On ten long Terminal-Bench 4.0 tasks, two agents on Claude Opus 5.5 score 93.33% vs 81.67% for Claude Code, in 64.77 minutes vs 130.75, and cheaper ($17.91 vs $20.31); with GPT-6-Astra they are more accurate but more expensive ($13.65 vs $9.01). Reading the shared context costs 1.7–3.7% of spend on Opus 5.5 and 7.7–17.7% on GPT-6-Astra; without any one of the coordination mechanisms the speed-up disappears. The ten tasks were selected for long Codex run times; with four agents, Terminal-Bench 4.0 accuracy drops by 3–4 points.

When distributed knowledge does not add up

A second difficulty for swarm memory is not length but distribution. If facts are spread across agents, the swarm has to gather them, and for that someone has to ask what the others know. Two benchmarks from 2026 conferences (ICML and ACL) expose different failures of knowledge exchange: in HiddenBench, agents fail to elicit others’ unique facts; in Silo-Bench, they often answer before gathering the data or fail to converge. A third work adds a direct comparison of shared and private memory. An engineering view of the same problems comes from a Caura.ai and Ben-Gurion University group (table at the end of the chapter): the shared memory of an agent fleet breaks in four ways, and an evaluation of their production service recovered all provenance chains of depth 4 but found an isolation bypass and a conflict in the order of checks.

experiment with LLM agentsecho & diversitybeliefs & consensusmemory3–4 agents in the main test; ablation up to 7

Systematic Failures in Collective Reasoning under Distributed Information in Multi-Agent LLMs (HiddenBench)

Carnegie Mellon University; Institute of Science Tokyo · May 2025

When facts are spread across agents, groups answer correctly 30.1% of the time vs 80.7% for one agent with all the facts; forcing all facts to be revealed in the first round raises accuracy to 92.6–98.2%.

More
Authors
Yuxuan Li, Aoi Naito, Hirokazu Shirado
Organizations
Carnegie Mellon University; Institute of Science Tokyo
Published
15 May 2025
Status
arXiv preprint; accepted at ICML 2026
Source
arxiv.org/abs/2505.11556

Yuxuan Li, Aoi Naito and Hirokazu Shirado (Carnegie Mellon), in "Systematic Failures in Collective Reasoning under Distributed Information in Multi-Agent LLMs" (HiddenBench, ICML 2026), took the "hidden profile" task from social psychology: each agent has shared facts and its own unique piece, and the correct answer is visible only to someone who puts everything together. On 65 tasks, groups of 3–4 agents (GPT-4o, 4.1 and 5, Gemini 2.5, Qwen3, Llama 4) answer correctly 30.1% of the time vs 80.7% for a single agent with the full set of facts. Forcing all facts to be revealed in the first round raises accuracy to 92.6% for GPT-4.1 and 98.2% for Gemini-2.5-Flash: agents can combine facts, but they do not ask on their own what the others know. For GPT-4.1, adding agents does not help (a gain from discussion of +0.348 with three, +0.006 with seven). A short "exchange facts, then decide" protocol (Exchange-Decide) gives 0.73–0.80. Models from 2024–2025, no Claude.

Silo-Bench: A Scalable Environment for Evaluating Distributed Coordination in Multi-Agent LLM Systems

Beijing University of Technology; Zhejiang University; Meituan; ETH Zürich · Mar 2026

When data are split across agents, the success of DeepSeek-V3.1 teams falls from 61.2% with two agents to 18.1% with a hundred; agents often submit an answer before collecting the data.

More
Authors
Yuzhe Zhang, Feiran Liu, Yi Shan, Xinyi Huang, Xin Yang et al. (11 authors)
Organizations
Beijing University of Technology; Zhejiang University; Meituan; ETH Zürich
Published
1 Mar 2026
Status
arXiv preprint; accepted at ACL 2026 (main conference)
Source
arxiv.org/abs/2603.01045

Yuzhe Zhang and colleagues at the Beijing University of Technology, Zhejiang University, Meituan and ETH Zürich, in "Silo-Bench: A Scalable Environment for Evaluating Distributed Coordination in Multi-Agent LLM Systems" (ACL 2026), distribute data across 2–100 agents of one model (DeepSeek-V3.1, GPT-OSS-120B, Qwen3-Next-80B-A3B): each holds its own piece, and every agent must output the exact answer to an algorithmic task. Mean success is 36.9% for DeepSeek, 16.9% and 8.2% for the others; for DeepSeek it falls from 61.2% with two agents to 18.1% with a hundred, and on tasks with a full data shuffle it is zero at 50 or more agents, while a single agent with all the data solves them 26.7% of the time (GPT-OSS; its 50-agent team: 0%). Agents build fitting structures on their own (a star, a chain of neighbours), but submit an answer before collecting the data (37.2% of analysed runs) or fail to converge (29.9%). Each configuration was run once, the models are open, and the tasks are exact computations.

Scaling Teams or Scaling Time? Memory Enabled Lifelong Learning in LLM Multi-Agent Systems

Emory University; Illinois Institute of Technology; Univ. of Notre Dame; Cisco Research · Mar 2026

Growing a team from 1 to 7 agents raises the composite score unevenly (Claude Sonnet 4.5: from 57.78 to 62.21), and in an ablation shared memory was slightly worse than private memory (48.62 vs 49.43).

More
Authors
Shanglin Wu, Yuyang Luo, Yueqing Liang, Kaiwen Shi, Yanfang Ye et al. (7 authors)
Organizations
Emory University; Illinois Institute of Technology; Univ. of Notre Dame; Cisco Research
Published
27 Mar 2026
Status
arXiv preprint
Source
arxiv.org/abs/2604.03295

Shanglin Wu, Kai Shu and colleagues at Emory, Illinois Tech, Notre Dame and Cisco Research, in "Scaling Teams or Scaling Time? Memory Enabled Lifelong Learning in LLM Multi-Agent Systems" (preprint, 27 March 2026), compare team size with accumulating experience through LLMA-Mem memory on MultiAgentBench. On 16 research tasks, teams of 1, 3, 5 and 7 agents give Claude Sonnet 4.5 a composite AAS score (the mean over a task sequence of the running mean; the score also includes a communication rating) of 57.78 / 58.96 / 62.57 / 62.21: it rises overall, but five agents score slightly above seven, and for Qwen3-32B three agents (59.21) beat five (58.45); tokens grow at every step, for Claude from 53.5 thousand to 344 thousand per task. The memory cuts spend by 9.4–71.7% relative to other memory systems, not relative to working without memory. The key result: in an ablation (code, Qwen3-32B) a shared store was slightly worse than private memory (48.62 vs 49.43), and a hybrid was lower still (46.92). The ceiling is seven agents.

Population memory and environment memory

Memory can also be kept outside the agents: in an archive of other agents' trajectories, from which a new agent retrieves relevant experience, or in the world itself, where traces and structures remain. A CMU and Berkeley group tested the first approach, an MIT lab the second, on societies of up to 200 agents. Both yield gains, but neither measures how such memory grows with the number of agents. Other forms of shared memory (the Kosmos "world model", AgentFlow's growing memory shared by roles, and the exchange of hidden vectors in Latent Collaboration) are collected in the table at the end of the chapter.

experiment with LLM agentsmemoryecho & diversityeconomics & scale34 models in both roles; number of unique agents not given; shared trajectory archive

Multi-Agent Transactive Memory

Carnegie Mellon University; UC Berkeley · Jun 2026

A shared index of trajectories from dozens of models raises success on ALFWorld from 0.4708 to 0.6431, but on WebArena only from 0.1818 to 0.2045; this is population memory, not a live swarm.

More
Authors
To Eun Kim, Xuhong He, Dishank Jain, Ambuj Agrawal, Negar Arabzadeh et al. (6 authors)
Organizations
Carnegie Mellon University; UC Berkeley
Published
18 Jun 2026
Status
arXiv preprint
Source
arxiv.org/abs/2606.19911

To Eun Kim, Fernando Diaz, Negar Arabzadeh and colleagues at CMU and Berkeley, in "Multi-Agent Transactive Memory" (preprint, 18 June 2026), pool trajectories of dozens of different models into a shared index. The next agent retrieves a piece of past experience from that index instead of living through the earlier run. On ALFWorld mean success rises from 0.4708 without retrieval to 0.6431 with an SVMRank reranker, steps fall from 11.77 to 10.35, and success grows monotonically with index size; on WebArena the rise is only from 0.1818 to 0.2045, and the size curve dips at half the index and reaches 20.9% on the full one. When retrieving only from other task types, ALFWorld gives 0.5985. This is population memory, not a live swarm: 77.82% of the ALFWorld index consists of trajectories of a trained seq2seq model from the benchmark's authors, and the contribution of this source is not measured; variances are not reported.

experiment with LLM agentsself-organizationmemoryeconomics & scale200 agents in one world; model calls once every 50 ticks

SwarmWorld: Stigmergic technological evolution in societies of language-model agents

Massachusetts Institute of Technology · Aug 2026

In a shared environment where up to 200 agents leave structures, shared worlds yield more validated inventions (5.75 and 7.00 vs 2.75), but the strongest single artefact stays with isolated search.

arxiv.org/abs/2608.26081

Subhadeep Pal, Fiona Y. Wang and Markus J. Buehler (MIT), in "SwarmWorld: Stigmergic technological evolution in societies of language-model agents" (preprint, 26 August 2026), put identical gpt-5.6-luna agents without roles into a shared 72×54 grid. The agents leave structures that contain executable programs, and those programs are tested after the agents are removed. The largest society is 200 agents in one world. The memory is stigmergic: a trace left in the world, not a message sent to a teammate. By tick 3,200 (100 agents), shared worlds give portfolio resilience of 0.2474 and 0.2365 against 0.1794 for the control (the best of N isolated single agents with the same number of decisions) and 5.75 and 7.00 validated inventions vs 2.75, but the strongest single artefact stays with isolated search (0.3488 vs 0.2380). About 95% of first reuses of a technology began with an agent seeing the object in the world rather than receiving a message: the memory lives in the environment. Four seeds per condition, game physics, one model.

Takeaways

  • The native window grew to 1 million tokens, but reasoning over the whole window and robustness to length remain weak (Opus 4.6; LOCA-bench, Chroma): the niche for swarm memory is not fitting more in, but not losing the thread.
  • Memory beyond the window works without a swarm: one agent answers over corpora 22–40 times longer than its window (RLM), and a coding agent with files beats published records on four of five long-context benchmarks (Duke).
  • The memory of real swarms lives in external structure: a claim graph (Lean), a knowledge base with a curator agent (ART), a shared folder (CORAL), a commit graph (Agora), the environment (SwarmWorld). It carries leads between sessions, but in the ART campaign the detail was seen only by the agent that read the raw DNA.
  • Shared notes raise answer accuracy with diminishing returns: 0.53 / 0.70 / 0.74 for 1 / 10 / 100 agents (Ch. 4). Distributed facts do not add up: 30.1% vs 80.7% (HiddenBench), 61.2% → 18.1% from 2 to 100 agents (Silo-Bench); forced exchange of facts helps.
  • Open: no work measures how swarm memory grows with the number of participants at hundreds of agents with a shared store; in the only direct comparison of shared and private memory within a team, private memory is slightly better (49.43 vs 48.62, ablation).

Related work

    Across 18 models from 2025: the longer the input, the less reliable the answer, and unevenly across positions
