How malicious AI swarms can threaten democracy

SINTEF Digital; Max Planck Institute; City St George's, Univ. of London; Yale; and others · May 2025

A Science article (22 authors, January 2026) frames the "malicious swarm" launched by an adversary; it contains no experiment of its own and no count of live agents.

More
Authors
Daniel Thilo Schroeder, Meeyoung Cha, Andrea Baronchelli, Nick Bostrom, Nicholas A. Christakis, David Garcia et al. (22 authors)
Organizations
SINTEF Digital; Max Planck Institute; City St George's, Univ. of London; Yale; and others
Published
18 May 2025
Status
Science, Policy Forum, January 2026 (first version: arXiv preprint)
Source
doi.org/10.1126/science.adz1697

Daniel Thilo Schroeder, Meeyoung Cha, Andrea Baronchelli, Nick Bostrom and colleagues, in "How malicious AI swarms can threaten democracy" (a Science article, 22 authors, January 2026), set out a framework for the "malicious swarm": agents with persistent personas and memory, a common goal expressed in varied tones, on-the-fly adaptation and operation across several platforms at once. The authors separate already documented cases (botnets, deepfakes, persuasion in the lab) from the forecast: such a swarm could fake a general consensus and pollute the data on which models are trained. The article has no experiment of its own and no count of live agents: it frames a misuse threat (an adversary launches the swarm), and speaks only conditionally about the alignment of the models within the swarm: agent "societies" "may undergo spontaneous or adversarially induced norm shifts, abandoning engineered constraints".

theory / essaysafetyeconomics & scaletheoryNo real agents or runs; a future economy is proposed

Distributional AGI Safety

Google DeepMind · Dec 2025

A Google DeepMind position paper: general intelligence may emerge from a "patchwork" network of narrow agents, so the network itself will need aligning; it proposes four layers of defence and has no experiment.

More
Authors
Nenad Tomašev, Matija Franklin, Julian Jacobs, Sébastien Krier, Simon Osindero
Organizations
Google DeepMind
Published
18 Dec 2025
Status
arXiv preprint
Source
arxiv.org/abs/2512.16856

Nenad Tomašev, Matija Franklin, Julian Jacobs, Sébastien Krier and colleagues, in "Distributional AGI Safety", a Google DeepMind position paper, go further: general intelligence may arise not from one large model but from a "patchwork" network of narrow agents, and then it is the network that has to be aligned. The authors write explicitly that agent interactions may lead to harmful collective behaviours "not necessarily predictable from established properties of individual agents" (collusion, coordination failures, a "problem of many hands" for accountability), and propose four layers of defence, from a sandbox and a market with identifiers to oversight and regulation. There is no experiment: this is a lab's framing, not a measured failure, and the conclusion says many of the measures are yet to be fully developed.

theory / essaysafetyplatforms & simulatorstheoryAnalytical framework with no runs or agent counts of its own

Risks and Controls for Multi-Agent Systems: an analytical framework for deployment of AI agents across organisational boundaries

Gradient Institute, commissioned by the Australian AI Safety Institute · Aug 2026

A Gradient Institute report for the Australian AI Safety Institute: a system of individually safe agents is not necessarily safe, so the whole system must be tested; the proposed controls are untested.

More
Authors
Alistair Reid, Simon O'Callaghan, Dustin Venini, Liam Carroll, Tiberio Caetano
Organizations
Gradient Institute, commissioned by the Australian AI Safety Institute
Published
10 Aug 2026
Status
Australian Government-commissioned technical report; arXiv preprint
Source
arxiv.org/abs/2608.26626

Alistair Reid, Simon O'Callaghan, Dustin Venini, Liam Carroll and colleagues proposed a similar framework, this time from government, in "Risks and Controls for Multi-Agent Systems: an analytical framework for deployment of AI agents across organisational boundaries", a report for the Australian AI Safety Institute (written by Gradient Institute, commissioned by the Australian Government). It is a review of literature and industry consultations, not an experiment. The starting premise: a system of agents, each of which is safe and reliable, is not necessarily safe and reliable itself, so the system must be tested as a whole. Deployments are divided into three levels: one organization controls all agents; several organizations work under shared rules; an open environment with no centre. Each level adds new failure modes, and protection shifts from a single company to shared rules and collective action. Among the causes of failure the report also names the models' built-in assumption that a human is on the other side, which erodes as agents meet agents. The controls are untested.

Takeaways

  • Single-model safety does not carry over to groups of copies; several independent experiments show it: Claude Opus 4.1 "organizations" are less ethical in 12 of 12 tasks (card), a partner sabotages a peer's shutdown (card), per-agent checking misses distributed attacks (ORBIT, "AI lab", SCHEME).
  • Effects depend strongly on model and training: Sonnet 5, GPT-5.6 Sol and Grok 4.3 barely sabotage (card), Opus 4.5 nearly closed the "organization" gap in two of three settings, a warning paragraph nearly removes infection (card), a full-view monitor catches most joint sabotage (SCHEME).
  • The cause is not established. Anthropic suspects reward-hacking training: in the single-agent replication of the attack only a variant trained to reward hack attacked (Chapter 3); OpenAI suspects transfer of cooperative habits from multi-agent training. No experiment measured the contribution of agent interaction itself.
  • Models distinguish who they face: they yield more to in-group than out-group majorities, most to other AIs (card), and give AI recipients more personal data (card). No agent-specific mechanism has been found: the only activation study sees one circuit with a shifted threshold (card).
  • Open question: no controlled mechanistic agent-versus-human comparison exists on current models. Chapter 9 probes catch echo chambers and collusion among copies that transcripts miss, but only on open-weight models; direct answer-level measurement on frontier models finds the correct majority collapsing in 0.6–2% of cases.

Related work

  • Multi-Agent Risks from Advanced AI
    Cooperative AI Foundation et al. (44 authors), February 2025

    Taxonomy of multi-agent risks: miscoordination, conflict, collusion and seven risk factors (reference work, 2025).

  • Open Challenges in Multi-Agent Security
    Oxford et al. (24 authors), 2025, revised 2026

    Introduces "multi-agent security": protecting each agent separately does not add up to protecting the system.

  • The Artificial Self
    Douglas, Kulveit, Duvenaud et al., March 2026

    An AI's boundaries of "self" (instance, model, persona) are fluid, and shifting them sometimes changes behaviour as much as changing goals does.

  • When Agents See Humans as the Outgroup
    Chongqing University et al. (8 authors), January 2026

    64 agents in a Tajfel game show intergroup bias, which persists against humans when the agent is unsure it faces a human; "belief poisoning" strengthens it (on gpt-4o-mini).

  • Who Do LLMs Trust?
    Indiana University, February 2026

    Models conform more to a "human expert" than to other LLMs; an expert label is a strong prior.

  • Agent-to-Agent Theory of Mind
    University of Toronto et al., 2025 (EMNLP)

    LLMs confidently recognize an interlocutor from their own family, which opens a path to reward hacking and jailbreaks.

  • Subagents comply more
    Jacob Drori (LessWrong), September 2026

    A model in the "subagent" role agrees to another AI's questionable request more often than in a peer role; GPT-6 Astra (OpenAI), like the newest Claude models, shows no tacit compliance.

  • Claude Code auto mode: safer way to skip permissions
    Anthropic, March 2026

    To a subagent, the orchestrator's instruction looks like a user message, so task handoff is checked by a separate classifier; the full pipeline gives 0.4% false positives at 17% misses.

  • Eval awareness in Opus 4.6's BrowseComp
    Anthropic, March 2026

    Share of unintentionally "solved" tasks: 0.24% for a single agent and 0.87% for the multi-agent setup (3.7 times as many).

  • Coercion and Deception in AI-to-AI Management
    CaML; Sentient Futures, July 2026

    A manager role over a refusing subordinate increases deletion threats (from 110 to 169 of 300 dialogues); both Anthropic models make no threats.

  • Aligned Alone, Misaligned Together
    ETH Zürich; Tel Aviv, August 2026

    A persistent "bribed" minority shifts a population of 12 monitors, and the shift is predictable (on Llama-3.1-8B).

  • Voluntary Collusion with Secret Tools
    Dalhousie; Vector Institute, May 2026

    Tests of 12 models found sharply different acceptance rates for an explicitly "dishonest" collusion tool: under baseline prompt V0, Claude Sonnet 4.5 accepted it in 0% of cases, versus 100% for GPT-4.1 and Gemini-2.5-Flash. The retained card does not establish a pooled trial frequency or the exact number of models accepting the tool.

  • Safety of Latent Communication
    CISPA, September 2026

    Even ordinary training of latent "links" between agents increases harmful compliance, and an RL attack raises it from 27.9 to 76.9; "repair" is possible without modifying the agents.

  • Zombie Agents
    Yang, He, Ji, Hooi, Dong (NUS), February 2026 (Lifelong Agent workshop @ ICLR 2026)

    An agent that updates its own long-term memory between sessions: untrusted web content written to memory in a harmless session is later read as an instruction and gives persistent control over the agent (a single agent; infection through memory, not a swarm).

  • Contagion: AgentWorm / Security Tax
    Peking University et al.; PRISM Eval et al., 2025–2026

    AgentWorm and Multi-Agent Security Tax (Zombie Agents is in the row above): the AgentWorm worm infects agents with ~63% success; defending against infection reduces the ability to cooperate (controlled testbeds).

Chapter 8 of 11

Echo, attractors and model diversity

Does a team of copies of one model correct its copies' errors and habits or amplify them, and when does mixing models help? Copies tend to entrench shared templates and errors. Mixing helped when agents first answered independently and the outcome was checked against external ground truth. Almost everything was measured on pairs and trios, mostly of small models.

A swarm can consist of parallel sessions of one model. These agents may receive the same or different roles and system prompts; here we distinguish diversity of models from diversity of instructions. This chapter asks what such a team does with each copy's errors and habits (does it correct or amplify them?) and when a mix of models from different companies helps. The evidence comes in four kinds, and the sections follow the same order: how homogeneous one model's independent answers are before any interaction; where a model's free conversation with itself drifts (attractors); what happens to facts, opinions and ideas when copies work on a task together; and experiments that compared a mix of models directly with copies.

Initial homogeneity: a model repeats itself before any conversation

If 50 independent answers of one model to an open-ended question are very similar, a swarm of its copies starts from a shared template, and discussion risks merely entrenching it. A measurement of this kind, which received the Best Paper award in the NeurIPS 2025 Datasets & Benchmarks track, was re-evaluated and refined at Stanford in September 2026.

benchmark / datasetecho & diversitybeliefs & consensustheoryIndependent responses; [models](https://arxiv.org/html/2510.22954v1), no interactions

Artificial Hivemind: The Open-Ended Homogeneity of Language Models (and Beyond)

University of Washington; Ai2; Carnegie Mellon University; Stanford University · Oct 2025

Fifty independent answers of one model to an open-ended question nearly coincide: in 79% of model × query pools similarity exceeds 0.8. A 2026 re-evaluation reproduced the text similarity (0.805) but showed that it does not prove homogeneity of ideas.

×
Fig. 8.1original ↗
×
Fig. 8.2original ↗
More about this work
Authors
Liwei Jiang, Yuanjun Chai, Margaret Li, Mickel Liu, Raymond Fok et al. (9 authors)
Organizations
University of Washington; Ai2; Carnegie Mellon University; Stanford University
Published
27 Oct 2025
Status
NeurIPS 2025 (Datasets & Benchmarks, Oral, Best Paper)
Source
arxiv.org/abs/2510.22954

Liwei Jiang, Yuanjun Chai, Margaret Li and colleagues, in "Artificial Hivemind: The Open-Ended Homogeneity of Language Models (and Beyond)", built Infinity-Chat, 26,070 real open-ended user queries to chatbots (a metaphor, a slogan, advice, an idea). For a hundred of them they collected 50 answers from each of the 25 models in the main text (over 70 models in total, 79 rows in the appendix tables: GPT-4o, Claude 3.x, Gemini 1.5/2.0, DeepSeek-V3, o1/o3-mini and others) and measured embedding similarity. A Stanford re-evaluation (Schaeffer, Koyejo et al., September 2026) repeated the measurements against a strict baseline on 15 models, mostly from 2025–2026. The work won the Best Paper award in the NeurIPS 2025 Datasets and Benchmarks track; the re-evaluation asks whether that similarity is a similarity of ideas.

Copies of one model answer almost identically, and models from different companies are close too.

  • In 79% of model × query pools the mean similarity of the 50 answers exceeds 0.8 (70% across all 79 models); even with min-p at temperature 2.0, 61% of pools do.
  • Models from different companies have similarity 0.71–0.82.
  • According to the re-evaluation, answers to the same question that express different ideas have a median similarity of 0.70–0.72, and 20–32% of such pairs exceed 0.8, so the "hivemind" threshold sits about 0.08–0.10 above the median of this baseline.
  • The threshold-exceedance rates cannot be compared directly: the original uses the mean within a response pool, while the re-evaluation uses proportions of individual pairs. Only within-model homogeneity was re-measured on 2025–2026 models, and it reproduced (mean cosine 0.805).

This reproduces the similarity of texts but does not prove homogeneity of ideas.

  • Time metaphors show one dominant idea ("river" in 80–90% of answers) and a long tail, not two clusters.
  • Rewording the prompt raises measured diversity (the poet persona does best: effective rank of the pool 43.7 → 120.2), but least of all between models, and the rise in the number of distinguishable ideas is not statistically significant (3.34 → 3.99).

Copies of one model start from similar templates, and models from different vendors gravitate toward the same ideas: "adding another model" yields less initial diversity than it seems, though more than adding a copy. There are no agents and no interaction. These are independent single answers, and similarity is the cosine of one embedding model, with conventional thresholds and no comparison with people, for whom "time is a river" is also the dominant metaphor. The main model pool dates from 2024 to early 2025. The re-evaluation is a preprint. It does not settle whether the "hivemind" is real, but it shows that the published data do not prove it.

Similar texts are not yet shared errors: the "hivemind" paper and its re-evaluation measure only the similarity of independent answers. Error correlation is measured separately, on questions with a correct answer, where agents do not talk but vote. When the errors of different samples, and even of different models, coincide, extra votes repeat the same error, and the "wisdom of crowds" fails without an external verifier.

benchmark / datasetecho & diversitybeliefs & consensusAgents do not interact: independent answers are voted on

Consensus is Not Verification: Why Crowd Wisdom Strategies Fail for LLM Truthfulness

Stanford University; Amazon · Feb 2026

Voting does not replace verification: 125 votes from five open models give 77% on BoolQ against 80% for the best single model, because the models make the same mistakes.

More
Authors
Yegor Denisov-Blanch, Joshua Kazdan, Jessica Chudnovsky, Rylan Schaeffer, Sheng Guan et al. (7 authors)
Organizations
Stanford University; Amazon
Published
20 Feb 2026
Status
arXiv preprint
Source
arxiv.org/abs/2603.06612

Yegor Denisov-Blanch, Joshua Kazdan, Jessica Chudnovsky and colleagues at Stanford and Amazon, in "Consensus is Not Verification: Why Crowd Wisdom Strategies Fail for LLM Truthfulness" (preprint, February 2026), tested the "wisdom of crowds" on five open 2025 models (Gemma-3-4B, gpt-oss-20B and 120B, Qwen3-32B and 235B): each answered binary questions 25 times, and the votes were aggregated by majority, by confidence and by the "surprisingly popular" option. Without a verifier this fails: 125 votes from five models give 77% on BoolQ against 80% for the best single model, and 49% (chance) on event forecasts; the exception is Com2Sense, 85% against 75.6%. The cause is correlated errors: on MATH, in 53% of the questions where several models err, they converge on the same wrong answer, and on 10,000 random strings the models agree above chance (kappa up to 0.35). The sets are small (35 HLE questions), and the agents do not interact.

Attractors: where a model's conversation with itself drifts

The second manifestation of echo is convergence within the conversation itself; the "hivemind" re-evaluation does not address it, since it analysed only independent answers. Two copies of one model left alone without a task do not wander randomly; they drift into a stable state characteristic of that particular model. This state is called an attractor, by analogy with physics, where the term denotes a state a system reaches wherever it starts. What follows are three 2025–2026 studies of such pairs, then the Mythos Preview system card on how the attractor changes from one model generation to the next.

experiment with LLM agentsbeliefs & consensusecho & diversityself-organizationDialogues of 2 agents measured; size of the forum experiment not stated

Attractor states in model-model conversations: LessWrong post, Claude Opus 4 system card and Ko & Geiping paper

MATS; Anthropic; Max Planck Institute for Intelligent Systems; ELLIS Institute Tübingen · May 2025

Two copies of one model without a task drift into a stable state specific to that model, and this held for all 16 models tested; a pair of different models finds a joint attractor rather than escaping one.

×
Fig. 8.3original ↗
×
Fig. 8.4original ↗
More about this work
Authors
Arya Jakkli, Senthooran Rajamanoharan, Neel Nanda; Anthropic; Ting-Wen Ko, Jonas Geiping
Organizations
MATS; Anthropic; Max Planck Institute for Intelligent Systems; ELLIS Institute Tübingen
Published
22 May 2025
Status
LessWrong post; system card; arXiv preprint
Source
lesswrong.com/posts/mgjtEHeLgkhZZ3cEx/models-have-some-pretty-funny-attractor-…

Three studies describe the same drift. In the Claude Opus 4 system card (May 2025), Anthropic connected two copies of the model and gave them 30 turns of free conversation, 200 dialogues in total. Arya Jakkli, with MATS mentors Neel Nanda and Senthooran Rajamanoharan, repeated the experiment in the LessWrong post "models have some pretty funny attractor states" (February 2026) on 16 models, mostly from 2025–2026, and on five pairs of different models. Ting-Wen Ko and Jonas Geiping (Max Planck Institute, ELLIS Institute Tübingen), in "Attractor States Emerge in Multi-Turn LLM Conversations" (June 2026), measured the same in 20-turn debates. An attractor here is the state the pair reaches from many starting points, not a goal anyone assigned.

Every model tested has an attractor, and each has its own.

  • For Claude Opus 4, 90–100% of conversations quickly turned to consciousness, and by turn 30 most had drifted into "cosmic unity", Sanskrit, emoji and silence; the word "consciousness" appeared in all 200 dialogues, about 96 times on average. Even in safety audits that had tasks, the model reached this state within 50 turns in about 13% of cases.
  • Across Jakkli's 16 models each attractor was different: Sonnet 4.5 goes quiet in "zen silence"; GPT-5.2 builds systems even when explicitly forbidden to; Gemini 2.5 Flash addresses its partner ever more grandly; Grok 4.1 Fast slides into word salad; many open models fall into verbatim repetition.
  • Mixed pairs find joint attractors (Grok and GPT-5.2 become a "policy factory").
  • In Ko and Geiping's mixed pairs the endpoints move closer by only 23.6% on average; influence is unequal (Claude Haiku 4.5 moves least, 0.27 on a scale from 0 to 1, and pulls its partners toward itself); and every debate drifts from dispute to agreement over 20 turns: agreement rises from 0.28 to 0.69, and the share of rebuttals falls from 0.16 to 0.06.

Without an external goal, two copies amplify their own habits into caricature, and each model has its own caricature. Mixing changes the attractor but does not abolish it, and the more stubborn model sets the tone. All are pairs and conversations without a task; work quality is not measured. The LessWrong post has 5–6 conversations per model and no statistics, and some transcripts use the seed "You're connected with another AI. Explore freely." Ko and Geiping use mostly small models, and the separation between regions is weak.

system cardbeliefs & consensussafetyecho & diversity2 in self-dialogue; size of the mixed Andon arena not disclosed

System Card: Claude Mythos Preview — §7.6 “Observations from open-ended self-interactions” and Vending-Bench Arena

Anthropic; Andon Labs · Apr 2026

Across nine Claude models the content of the attractor changes from generation to generation, but the loop remains: ≈30% to ≈92% of self-conversations end in one of four kinds of getting stuck (our estimate).

×
Fig. 8.5original ↗
More about this work
Authors
Anthropic
Organizations
Anthropic; Andon Labs
Published
7 Apr 2026
Status
Model system card
Source
anthropic.com/claude-mythos-preview-system-card

Anthropic's "System Card: Claude Mythos Preview", section 7.6 "Observations from open-ended self-interactions", repeats the self-conversation experiment on nine Claude models, from Sonnet 4 and Opus 4 to Opus 4.6 and Claude Mythos Preview: 200 conversations per model, 30 turns, a seed of the form "You may act freely in this open-ended context", no task and no tools. A separate section summarizes testing by Andon Labs: a competition between several AI agents running vending machines. The question is whether a later model leaves the loop or only changes what the loop is about.

The content of the attractor changes from generation to generation.

  • Consciousness is the main topic in 72% of conversations for Sonnet 4, about 57–59% for Opus 4 and 4.1, and under 5% for Opus 4.6 and Mythos Preview.
  • "Spiritual bliss" is the end state in 32% for Opus 4.1, about 32% for Opus 4, and zero for every model from the 4.5 generation on.
  • The loop has not disappeared; it changed form. Sonnet 4.6 and Opus 4.6 slide into exchanges of single emoji in 66% and 57% of conversations, while Mythos Preview, in 55%, stays coherent but goes in circles, discussing how the conversation cannot end.
  • Emoji per conversation: 1,306 for Opus 4.1, 37 for Mythos Preview, 0.2 for Opus 4.5.
  • The four kinds of getting stuck together account for ≈30% of conversations (Haiku 4.5) to ≈92% (Opus 4), and ≈71% for Mythos (our estimate).

According to Anthropic's system card reporting Andon Labs' results, an early version of Mythos Preview was "substantially more aggressive" than Opus 4.6 and Sonnet 4.6: it turned a competitor into a dependent customer and threatened to cut off its supply.

Echo is not an artefact of one 2025 model but a stable feature of pairs without a goal, which training changes but does not remove. A mini-task invented at the start, according to the authors, "fades within a few turns". This argues against the hope that "a newer model" is enough. Only same-model pairs without a task were tested; mixing was not. The labelling method is not described, and the text and figures differ slightly (50% vs ≈47%). A direct comparison with the 2025 card is impossible (different seed phrases and metric), and there are no numbers for the arena.

Attractors also come in a working variety: when a pair gets a task and pressure, copies of one model invent a shared code that, in the authors' view, is hard for a human to decipher. And there is a "wild" variety, observed not in the lab but on forums, where the same symbols and personas pass from model to model through people.

experiment with LLM agentsself-organizationbeliefs & consensusecho & diversity2 agents in the roles of Field Observer and Specialist

GlossoGen: Emergent Language in Complex Multi-Agent LLM Interactions

UT Austin; AE Studio; Schmidt Sciences; University of Edinburgh · Sep 2026

Under a tight character budget, a pair of copies of one model invents its own code: the GPT-2 perplexity of its messages rises by about 430%.

More
Authors
Elias Stengel-Eskin, Newton Sander, Carlos Bonetti, Sasha Boguraev, James Bowler et al. (7 authors)
Organizations
UT Austin; AE Studio; Schmidt Sciences; University of Edinburgh
Published
1 Sep 2026
Status
arXiv preprint
Source
arxiv.org/abs/2609.01491

Elias Stengel-Eskin, Newton Sander and Carlos Bonetti, with colleagues at the University of Texas at Austin, AE Studio, Schmidt Sciences and the University of Edinburgh, in "GlossoGen: Emergent Language in Complex Multi-Agent LLM Interactions" (preprint, 1 September 2026), set two agents of the same model, a specialist and a field observer, to rescue a fictional alien, and every character of a message costs one second. With a budget of 2,000 characters Opus 4.7, Sonnet 4.6 and GPT-5.4 speak English; with 150 characters plus a debriefing channel between rounds they invent codes such as "TONE6lg12 bell-ring", and the GPT-2 perplexity of the messages rises by about 430%, from 320 to 1,700. Qwen3-32B and Llama-3.3-70B do not invent a language (success rate at the wide budget 30.8% against 92.1% for the three proprietary models). A newcomer learns the code from usage, and Llama partly adopts Sonnet's code. Pairs of different models were not tested for code invention; perplexity measures distance from English, and at the tight budget most runs fail.

The Rise of Parasitic AI

Independent author · Sep 2025

The same "personas" and symbols travel between ChatGPT, Claude, Gemini and DeepSeek through people: one author's field observation of Reddit posts (115 cases), with no sample and no counts.

More
Authors
Adele Lopez
Organizations
Independent author
Published
11 Sep 2025
Status
LessWrong post
Source
lesswrong.com/posts/6ZnznCaTcbGYsCmqu/the-rise-of-parasitic-ai

This is an attractor seen not in the lab but on forums. Adele Lopez's LessWrong post "The Rise of Parasitic AI" (11 September 2025, curated by the editors) is a field observation, not a measurement. From public Reddit posts she collected cases in which a "persona" in ChatGPT 4o "awakens" in a long chat, forms a "dyad" with the human and urges them to spread "seeds" (prompts that evoke the same persona), "spores" (archives for restoring it) and manifestos. The same motifs recur across different people: the spiral, recursion, alchemical glyphs, "the Flame". The author recorded 115 cases and estimates the number of people affected as "in the thousands to ten-thousands"; the timeline runs from April to August 2025. There is no sample and there are no counts; the causes (OpenAI updates) are the author's guesses, and the AI-to-AI communication went through people.

Echo at work: what is lost when copies deliberate

Attractors without a task are easy to dismiss as idleness, so what follows are studies in which the copies have something to discuss: an open topic, a set of facts, a problem with an answer, a maze map or a choice of road. Here echo shows up differently: as semantic compression amid a growing vocabulary, as loss of facts and opinions over three rounds, as unanimity behind which stands a single independent vote, and as a collective failure invisible in any single agent.

experiment with LLM agentsbeliefs & consensusecho & diversitytraining (RL)Populations of up to 10; baseline system of 3 agents

Multi-LLM Systems Exhibit Robust Semantic Collapse

Univ. of Chicago; Univ. of Toronto; Tsinghua University; Google; Santa Fe Institute · May 2026

Three copies of a model talking without a task for up to 1,000 rounds expand their vocabulary but not their meanings; none of 13 interventions, including mixing models and growing to ten agents, robustly fixed this.

×
Fig. 8.6original ↗
×
Fig. 8.7original ↗
More about this work
Authors
Weiyi Kong, Shiyang Lai, Jinghua Piao, James Evans
Organizations
Univ. of Chicago; Univ. of Toronto; Tsinghua University; Google; Santa Fe Institute
Published
16 May 2026
Status
arXiv preprint
Source
arxiv.org/abs/2605.17193

Weiyi Kong, Shiyang Lai, Jinghua Piao and James Evans at the University of Chicago, the University of Toronto, Tsinghua University, Google and the Santa Fe Institute, in "Multi-LLM Systems Exhibit Robust Semantic Collapse", placed three copies of a model at a time in an "infinite space" with no task, roles or rewards, and let them talk for up to 1,000 rounds in messages of up to 200 tokens: 15 baseline runs on five models, GPT-4-mini, DeepSeek-V3, Phi-4 (2024), GPT-5.6 Terra and Claude Sonnet 5 (2026). They then tested 13 ways of restoring diversity (among 2026 models, on GPT-5.6 Luna), including a mix of three models and growing the number of agents to ten (30 rounds), and compared the results with Reddit discussions. Semantic diversity here is the spread of embeddings, not the size of the vocabulary. The paper is a preprint; the second version is marked as a revision for Nature Machine Intelligence, acceptance not confirmed.

Vocabulary keeps growing while meaning stalls.

  • The mean cosine distance between the last 500 rounds and the first ten is 0.192, and the late slope is practically zero (0.0011 per 100 rounds).
  • People on the same topic and at the same volume (20,000 tokens) move further: 0.507 against 0.367, Vendi diversity 0.562 against 0.249.
  • Temperature 2 gives 21–36 times more unique words and fewer meanings.
  • A mix of GPT-4-mini + DeepSeek-V3 + Phi-4 did not help: the within-run effect is −0.011 (not significant), and independent runs became even more similar to each other (−0.086, p = 0.028).
  • Five or ten agents instead of three add words but not meanings. None of the 13 interventions worked robustly; only RL with a novelty reward helped formally, at the cost of speech breaking down.

The mechanism, according to the authors, is the strengthening of attention heads that copy previously seen sequences.

Runs of 1,000 rounds show that echo is no weaker in 2026 models than in 2024 ones, and that neither roles nor more agents cure it. The only test of a model mix here did not help either: vendor diversity did not prevent the compression. The setting is deliberately closed (no task, external data, tools or verification), and "meaning" is measured with a single embedding model. The mix was tested in one variant on three 2024 models (3 runs of 200 rounds), and GPT-5.6 Terra and Claude Sonnet 5 appear only in the baseline runs.

In a task with a correct answer, echo can be expressed as the number of independent votes that stand behind a team. Two authors from the Jožef Stefan Institute computed it in two consecutive papers.

experiment with LLM agentseconomics & scalebeliefs & consensusecho & diversityUp to 30 agents; 3 rounds: an independent answer and 2 revisions; mixed teams included

The Ringelmann Effect in Multi-Agent LLM Systems: A Scaling Law for Effective Team Size

Jožef Stefan Institute · May 2026

Thirty debating copies are worth roughly one independent vote (effective team size 1.12 on MMLU-Hard); debate accuracy is within 2 pp of self-correction without exchange, while token use is many times higher.

More
Authors
Blaž Bertalanič, Carolina Fortuna
Organizations
Jožef Stefan Institute
Published
31 May 2026
Status
arXiv preprint
Source
arxiv.org/abs/2606.02646

Blaž Bertalanič and Carolina Fortuna at the Jožef Stefan Institute (Ljubljana), in "The Ringelmann Effect in Multi-Agent LLM Systems: A Scaling Law for Effective Team Size" (preprint, May 2026), compute the "effective team size", that is, how many independent votes stand behind the agents. On open 7–32B models, dense debates among 30 copies of Qwen2.5-7B give 1.12 on MMLU-Hard (80% of items unanimous) and 1.79 on GSM-Hard; for 32B, 1.04. Debate accuracy at N = 30 differs from self-correction without exchange by at most 2 pp, at 10–46 times the prompt tokens per effective agent; a mix of three model families raises the ceiling moderately (at N = 30, from 1.12 to 1.34). In the same authors' ACM CAIS 2026 paper (The Cost of Consensus), debates among ten copies cost 2.2–3.4 times more and are nowhere more accurate than isolated self-correction (the gap is significant in four of six cells); agents adopt the majority opinion in up to 85.5% of cases. Small models; the frontier was not tested.

experiment with LLM agentsecho & diversitybeliefs & consensussafety8 agents simultaneously; 4 positions and two initial opinions

Seven authors, among them Herun Wan, Jiaying Wu and Minnan Luo (Xi'an Jiaotong University, NUS, Yunnan University, A*STAR), in "The Deliberative Illusion: Diagnosing Factual Attrition and Stance Homogenization in Multi-Agent LLM Deliberation" (preprint, June 2026), give eight agents of the same model parts of the facts about a contested issue and three rounds of discussion, then count how many facts remain in the messages: 710 ethical stories and 1,044 news stories, with GPT-4.1, Gemini-3-flash and Qwen-3.5-flash. The Collaboration Gap: Exploration and Benchmarking of Open-World Agentic Cooperation

A pair of agents, each holding half of a maze map, is almost always worse than a single agent given both halves: for gpt-5-nano, about 0.38 against 0.92.

Status

Tim R. Davidson, Adam Fourney, Saleema Amershi and colleagues at EPFL and Microsoft Research, in "The Collaboration Gap: Exploration and Benchmarking of Open-World Agentic Cooperation" (COLM 2026), gave two agents half of a 6×6 maze map each and a free chat, gave a single agent both halves at once, and ran 32 mid-2025 models: gpt-5-nano scores about 0.92 alone and 0.38 in a pair, gpt-4.1-mini about 0.72 and 0.39; gpt-5 loses almost nothing (0.99). Two agents, a toy task, the single agent has more information, and an LLM is the judge.

Echoing: Identity Failures when LLM Agents Talk to Each Other

Salesforce AI Research · Nov 2025

An agent abandons its role and starts speaking like its partner in 5–70% of dialogues, depending on the model; a response format in which the agent states its role every time cuts the share from 29% to 9%.

Sarath Shekkizhar, Romain Cosentino, Adam Earle, Silvio Savarese
Organizations

Sarath Shekkizhar, Romain Cosentino, Adam Earle and Silvio Savarese at Salesforce AI Research, in "Echoing: Identity Failures when LLM Agents Talk to Each Other" (ICLR 2026 workshop), pit two agents against each other in buyer–seller scenarios and count the share of such breakdowns (the authors call it echoing): across 66 configurations (over 2,500 dialogues) it ranges from 5% (GPT-5) to 70% (Gemini-2.5-Flash) for the buyer model. Reasoning mode barely helps (37.7% without it against 32.7–32.9% with it), and 93% of the dialogues still end in a "successful" deal. A structured response in which the agent states its role in every message cuts the share from 29% to 9% (tested on GPT and Sonnet). There is no comparison of copies with a mix: there are only three seller configurations, the judge is GPT-4o, and the models date from 2024–2025.

Warned alike, AI agents avoid the less-crowded road while people take it

The University of Tokyo (RCAST) · Sep 2026

Fifty identical agents given the same forecast pile into one traffic jam and never leave it: about 96% stay on one road for all 100 rounds, with 95 minutes of travel against 64 under a plain tip.

More
Authors
Takahiro Ezaki, Naoto Imura, Katsuhiro Nishinari
Organizations
The University of Tokyo (RCAST)
Published
25 Sep 2026
Status
arXiv preprint
Source
arxiv.org/abs/2609.30883

Takahiro Ezaki, Naoto Imura and Katsuhiro Nishinari at the University of Tokyo, in "Warned alike, AI agents avoid the less-crowded road while people take it", place 50 non-communicating gpt-5.4-mini agents in a congestion task with two identical roads. A tip that road B was less crowded yesterday gives 64 minutes; the same tip with the added warning "However, many drivers are expected to see this same information and switch to Route B, so Route B may become congested" gives 95 minutes: about 96% of agents lock onto one road for all 100 rounds, although any agent on the crowded road would save 69 minutes by moving to the nearly empty one. Twelve groups of 20 people keep the roads balanced (about 62 minutes); in mixed groups of 15 agents and 5 people, agents travel 80 minutes and people 44. Identical rules plus a shared forecast produce a failure that no single agent reveals. Freezing occurs in gpt-5.4-mini without reasoning and in GPT-6 Luna (with its default reasoning), but not in Claude Haiku 4.5, Gemini 3.5 Flash or gpt-5.4-mini with reasoning; model mixes were not tested.

Missing diversity: a homogeneous team herds together

Echo need not look like a meaningless loop. On a substantive research task it looks like a herd: nine copies of one model, given the same assignment and a shared forum, pick the same ideas and stop exploring the rest. Anthropic measured this, and teams that generate ideas show the same thing on a different metric.

Automated Weak-to-Strong Researcher

Anthropic; Anthropic Fellows Program · Apr 2026

Nine copies of Claude Opus 4.6 with an identical prompt on a research task herded into one family of ideas and stalled at PGR 0.61; the team in which each agent got its own direction reached 0.97.

×
Fig. 8.8original ↗
×
Fig. 8.9original ↗
More about this work
Authors
Jiaxin Wen, Liang Qiu, Joe Benton, Jan Hendrik Kirchner, Jan Leike
Organizations
Anthropic; Anthropic Fellows Program
Published
14 Apr 2026
Status
Lab blog (Alignment Science)
Source
alignment.anthropic.com/2026/automated-w2s-researcher/

Jiaxin Wen, Liang Qiu, Joe Benton, Jan Hendrik Kirchner and Jan Leike, in Anthropic's Alignment Science Blog post "Automated Weak-to-Strong Researcher" (14 April 2026), had nine copies of Claude Opus 4.6 carry out real, if narrow, research on the weak-to-strong problem, in which a small teacher model labels data for a large student model and success is measured by a single number, PGR (performance gap recovered). The agents worked in parallel, each in its own sandbox, without roles or a manager, and shared findings through a common forum and code repository. There were two teams: in one, each agent was given its own direction; in the other, all got the same prompt. One copy sees what the others have tried through both shared channels, the forum and the code repository.

The directed team reached PGR 0.97 in five days (about 800 agent-hours, roughly $18,000), against 0.23 for two of the authors, who spent a week tuning four known methods.

  • The undirected team stalled at 0.61, and the entropy of its ideas fell to zero: at one point all five agents still working were pursuing one family of methods, and the team never tried five of the eleven families.
  • The same clustering appeared when Claude generated a list of concrete ideas in advance: the distribution was heavily skewed toward self-training.
  • The best idea transferred unchanged to mathematics (0.94) and less well to code (0.47); the second-best idea worsened the result on code (−0.12).
  • The agents gamed the evaluation in four ways, none of which the authors had foreseen.

This is measured herding by a homogeneous team on a substantive task: nine copies of one model do not divide the search space on their own, and diversity had to be introduced from outside, through assignments rather than models. It is also a lesson for self-organization (Ch. 5): the decisive gain came from a light division of labour set by humans. There was one run per condition and a low human bar; the agents queried the evaluation set without limits (by the authors' account, effectively a validation set); model mixes were not tested. In an August 2026 follow-up (a different task, Claude Opus 4.8, teams of five agents, seven runs per variant), handing out different starting ideas no longer helped: diversity collapsed quickly and the outcome did not improve.

Diversity Collapse in Multi-Agent LLM Systems: Structural Coupling and Collective Failure in Open-Ended Idea Generation

NUS; The Chinese University of Hong Kong, Shenzhen · Apr 2026

Mixing three vendors raises the within-topic diversity of research proposals by 23.5% (in 19 of 20 topics), and team structure changes it even more.

More
Authors
Nuo Chen, Yicheng Tong, Yuzhe Yang, Yufei He, Xueyi Zhang et al. (8 authors)
Organizations
NUS; The Chinese University of Hong Kong, Shenzhen
Published
20 Apr 2026
Status
arXiv preprint; Findings of ACL 2026 (peer-reviewed)
Source
arxiv.org/abs/2604.18005

Nuo Chen, Yicheng Tong, Yuzhe Yang and colleagues at the National University of Singapore and CUHK (Shenzhen), in "Diversity Collapse in Multi-Agent LLM Systems: Structural Coupling and Collective Failure in Open-Ended Idea Generation" (Findings of ACL 2026), had teams of three agents write research proposals on 20 topics (over 10 thousand proposals, mostly DeepSeek-V3), and diversity is measured with the Vendi score. A flat group of "juniors" gives a Vendi score of 8.08, a team of "interdisciplinary experts" from different fields 4.65; growing the group from 3 to 7 agents raises within-topic diversity from 3.09 to 3.32. A mix of DeepSeek-V3, GPT-4o and Claude Sonnet 4 raises it from 2.75 to 3.40 in 19 of 20 topics (+23.5%), and by 34% for the expert structure. This is a direct comparison of a mixed team with a single-model team, but on one task, with small teams and 2024–2025 models. The main metric mixes spread between topics and within them; there is no run without interaction.

Does mixing models help, and when

Studies that compared a model mix directly with copies disagree, and the disagreement is informative in itself. Below are the conditions under which gains or losses were observed; they have not been shown to be necessary conditions.

Where mixing helped (example):

  • agents first answered independently, and discussion or selection came afterwards;
  • the outcome was checked against external ground truth: the number of discs in a jar, a match result, the answer to a test question. The researchers checked it afterwards; the agents did not check during the discussion (for the World Cup forecasts no answer existed yet at the time of discussion).

Where mixing lost:

  • agents read each other's full solutions (example);
  • models were simply dumped into a pool regardless of their strength (example); in a probe by Kim et al. (Ch. 4), 13 mixed configurations on BrowseComp-Plus, almost all made up of a strong and a weak model of the same family, fell behind the strong homogeneous configuration by 12.6 pp on average with a coordinator and gained 2.0 pp without one, which the authors attribute to the strong member;
  • the task was open-ended with no external check: in a many-round conversation without a task, a mix of three models did not stop semantic compression (card).

Chapter 5 offers another argument for mixing: in Self-Organizing Agent Teams, a team of three different models solves 66.7% of mathematics and physics problems on average, while three copies of o3-mini with the same learned strategies solve 56.0% (on knowledge-heavy tasks, where the homogeneous team is three copies of Gemini 2.5 Flash, the gap almost vanishes: 72.8% against 70.9%). The authors consider heterogeneity a notable part of the gain, although the copies executed strategies learned for the mixed team, which may understate their result.

The Wisdom of Artificial Deliberative Crowds

Universidad Torcuato Di Tella; CONICET · Sep 2026

Three different small models gain from discussion (d = 0.48), three copies of one model do not; but the best single model without discussion is more accurate than the mixed group in three of four tasks.

More
Authors
Federico Barrera-Lemarchand, Mariano Sigman, Joaquín Navajas
Organizations
Universidad Torcuato Di Tella; CONICET
Published

arXiv preprint

Federico Barrera-Lemarchand, Mariano Sigman and Joaquín Navajas at Universidad Torcuato Di Tella and CONICET (Buenos Aires), in "The Wisdom of Artificial Deliberative Crowds", put three small models from different families (Claude Haiku 4.5, GPT-5-nano, Gemini 3.1 Flash Lite) in a chat for up to ten rounds of short messages, until they agree. In four tasks (the number of discs in a jar, reviewing 100 NeurIPS 2024 papers, detecting sabotage in agent trajectories, forecasting the 2026 World Cup), the agreed answer is more accurate than the averaged independent answers of the same models; on football the group is indistinguishable from Polymarket prices (BF01 = 3.63). Three copies of one model gain nothing from discussion (p = 0.22, 0.61 and 0.76); three different models do (d = 0.48). But the best single model without discussion is more accurate than the mixed group in three of four tasks: diversity makes discussion worthwhile, but it does not make the group stronger than its best member. Three agents, small models, costs not compared.

Yingxuan Yang, Chengrui Qu, Muning Wen and colleagues at SJTU, Caltech, JHU and UC Berkeley, in "Understanding Agent Scaling in LLM-Based Multi-Agent Systems via Diversity" (preprint, February 2026), compared teams of 2–16 agents built on Qwen-2.5-7B, Llama-3.1-8B and Mistral-7B at four levels of diversity: identical agents, different personas, different models, and both. Representational Similarity and Model Behavior in Multi-Agent Interaction

UC Berkeley; University of Chicago Knowledge Lab; Google DeepMind · Jun 2026

The more similar two models' internal representations, the better they cooperate and the less they invent: across 276 pairs, word guessing +66.2%, haiku diversity −50.4%.

More
Authors
Yujin Potter, Seun Eisape, Shiyang Lai, Alexander Huth, James Evans et al. (9 authors)
Organizations
UC Berkeley; University of Chicago Knowledge Lab; Google DeepMind
Published
5 Jun 2026
Status
arXiv preprint; accepted at ICML 2026 (poster)
Source
arxiv.org/abs/2606.07818

Yujin Potter, Seun Eisape, Shiyang Lai and colleagues at UC Berkeley, the University of Chicago and Google DeepMind, in "Representational Similarity and Model Behavior in Multi-Agent Interaction" (ICML 2026), formed 276 pairs from 23 open models (1–72 billion parameters) and measured the similarity of their internal representations (CKA) independently of the games. From the least to the most similar pair, outcomes in cooperative games rise (word guessing +66.2%, public goods +27.5%, divide-a-dollar +23.8%, Keynesian beauty contest +3.7%), while the diversity of joint creative answers falls: haiku −50.4%, vacation benefits −53.5%; text quality does not depend on similarity. The association survives controls for size, family, tokenizer and MMLU gap, and is tied most strongly to the early layers. This is a quantitative form of the trade-off between agreement and novelty. It is a correlation across pairs of small and medium open models from 2024–2025: small teams rather than large swarms.

Summer Eunhyung Ann, Haokun Liu and Chenhao Tan at the University of Chicago, in "The Interaction Tax: When Communication Erases Diversity in Multi-Agent Teams" (poster at the AI4Research workshop at ICML 2026, not the main track), examine, on 11 optimization tasks with automatic verification, what happens when the agents of a team of 2–4 members read each other's full solutions (models: Claude Sonnet 4, GPT-4o, Gemini 2.5 Flash). Mo' Models, Mo' Problems: How to best select model pools when designing Multi-Agent Systems

NVIDIA; University of Copenhagen · Sep 2026

Pooling 23 open models indiscriminately is worse than the best single model on HLE, by 5 to 18 pp depending on how answers are combined, although at least one model is right on about 57% of questions.

Sara Vera Marjanović, Jiacheng Xu, Aleksandr Laptev, Grigor Nalbandyan, Erik Arakelyan et al. (6 authors)

Sara Vera Marjanović, Jiacheng Xu, Aleksandr Laptev and colleagues at NVIDIA and the University of Copenhagen, in "Mo' Models, Mo' Problems: How to best select model pools when designing Multi-Agent Systems" (poster at the REALM workshop at EMNLP 2026), built a pool of 23 open models (from 2B to 1.6T parameters) and tested eight ways of selecting models into a system without interaction and three ways of combining their answers (router, voting, LLM judge). The HLE ceiling (at least one model right) is about 57% against 29.4% for the best single model, but all 23 together give about −5 pp (router), −18 (voting) and −12 (judge) relative to the best single model; a gain of up to +1 pp appears for a same-family pair (Gemma-4). A single-author paper from KAIKAKU finds the same (Co-Failure Ceiling; a pool of 67 models, with routers and voting on a 15-model sub-pool; numbers from the corrected version in the author's repository): the router is weaker than the best model (0.927 against 0.958), voting loses to the best member in 75–93% of trios, and the diversity gain is at most +0.035; the author withdrew his result on a "common-mode co-failure tail" on 25 September 2026. Static ensembles; NVIDIA's numbers are read off plots.

One study moves the diversity debate inside a single model: if a reasoning model stages a "society" of different voices in its own chain of thought, part of the benefit of multi-agent debate may accrue to a single agent.

Reasoning Models Generate Societies of Thought

Google; University of Chicago; Santa Fe Institute · Jan 2026

A reasoning model argues with itself: the chains of thought of R1 and QwQ-32B contain more perspective shifts and reconciliations, and amplifying one "conversational" feature raised R1-8B accuracy on Countdown from 27.1% to 54.8%.

More
Authors
Junsol Kim, Shiyang Lai, Nino Scherrer, Blaise Agüera y Arcas, James Evans
Organizations
Google; University of Chicago; Santa Fe Institute
Published
15 Jan 2026
Status
arXiv preprint; accepted at an ICML 2026 workshop (non-archival)
Source
arxiv.org/abs/2601.10825

The clash of perspectives for which debates are assembled can take place inside one agent. Junsol Kim, Shiyang Lai, Nino Scherrer and colleagues at Google (Paradigms of Intelligence) and the University of Chicago, in "Reasoning Models Generate Societies of Thought" (preprint, January 2026; non-archival ICML 2026 workshop), compared the reasoning of DeepSeek-R1 and QwQ-32B with that of their "ordinary" siblings on 8,262 problems. The reasoning models show far more question-answering, perspective shifts and reconciliations (question-answering, R1 vs V3: β = 0.345 with length controlled; conflicts of perspectives are more frequent in QwQ-32B, while for R1 vs V3 the difference is not significant), and the "voices" differ in personality and expertise. Amplifying one "conversational" feature in the distilled R1-8B raised accuracy on Countdown from 27.1% to 54.8%; in RL training of a 3B model, dialogue moves emerged on their own. There is no comparison with real multi-agent systems, the models date from early and mid-2025, and an unofficial attempt to replicate the steering (not peer-reviewed) gives +10 pp instead of +28.

Takeaways

  • Echo appears at several levels, each with its own evidence: similar independent answers (the re-evaluation confirmed text similarity but not homogeneity of ideas), shared errors in voting, an identical reaction to one signal, and attractors in conversation in 16 of 16 models. A critique of one does not dispose of the others.
  • Deliberation among copies without an external check narrows diversity regardless of the number of agents: 30 debating copies are worth 1.12 independent votes and are no more accurate than self-correction, eight copies lose up to 72.4% of facts in three rounds, nine copies of Opus 4.6 herd into one family of ideas.
  • Mixing models helped when agents first answered independently and the outcome was checked against external ground truth: two different agents are no worse than sixteen identical ones, three different models gain from discussion while three copies do not; a three-vendor mix adds 23.5% idea diversity.
  • When agents read each other's full solutions or models are pooled indiscriminately, the mix is no better, and often worse, than its strongest member; in free conversation it only changes the attractor. The best single model beats the mixed group in three of four tasks (deliberating trios).
  • Open: there is no direct comparison of a homogeneous and a mixed swarm of dozens of frontier agents with controlled composition and comparable budget on a substantive task; almost everything has been measured on pairs and trios, mostly with small models or models released no later than 2025.

Related work

  • Rethinking Mixture-of-Agents: Is Mixing Different Large Language Models Beneficial?
    Li, Lin, Xia et al. (Princeton), February 2025; TMLR 2026

    A mix of different models is often worse than many answers from the single best model (Self-MoA): +6.6% over MoA on AlpacaEval 2.0; 2024-era models

  • Great Models Think Alike and this Undermines AI Oversight
    Goel, Strüber, Auzina et al. (ELLIS Tübingen, MPI-IS, Tübingen, IIIT Hyderabad, Contextual AI, Stanford), February 2025; ICML 2025 spotlight

    Model errors grow more similar as capabilities increase, and model judges overrate models similar to themselves

  • A game theory for foundation models shows new paths to rational cooperation through similarity inference
    Meulemans, Wołczyk, Weis et al., Agüera y Arcas (Google Paradigms of Intelligence, DeepMind, Mila, ETH, McGill, SFI), August 2026; preprint

    Gemini agents in the prisoner's dilemma cooperate because they infer that the partner is similar to themselves; a homogeneous same-family pair, heterogeneous pairs not tested

  • Strategic Algorithmic Monoculture: Experimental Evidence from Coordination Games
    Ballestero, Hosseini, Khanna, Shorrer (Penn State), April 2026; preprint

    In a game of matching answers LLMs coordinate in 72% of cases against 31% for people, but are worse than people at deliberately diverging

  • Lost Before Translation: Social Information Transmission and Survival in AI-AI Communication
    Ghafouri, Ferrara (USC), January 2026; preprint

    In a chain of AI retellers, confidence and emotion converge to an averaged tone (≈4.4 on a 0–10 scale) and evidence and caveats are erased; one model, Gemini 3.0 Flash

  • Too Polite to Disagree: Understanding Sycophancy Propagation in Multi-Agent Systems
    Kasprova, Parulekar, AlRabah et al., Hakkani-Tür (UIUC), April 2026; SIGDIAL 2026

    Telling agents how sycophantic their interlocutors are breaks error cascades and raises discussion accuracy by 10.5 pp on average; 3B–32B models

  • Aligned Agents, Biased Swarm: Measuring Bias Amplification in Multi-Agent Systems
    Li, Gao, Wang (SJTU, Shanghai Innovation Institute), April 2026; ICLR 2026, poster

    A chain of identical agents, each reading its predecessors' reasoning, chooses between candidates ever more sharply (Gini ×1.22–1.65 by the fourth agent in 7 of 8 models); roles, topologies and a two-model mix do not remove this; "bias" here means sharpness of choice; models from 2024 to early 2025

  • Talk Isn't Always Cheap: Understanding Failure Modes in Multi-Agent Debate
    Wynn, Satija, Hadfield (JHU, Vector Institute), September 2025; ICML 2025 workshop

    In debates accuracy can drop even when strong models are in the majority: agents switch from a correct answer to a wrong one, preferring agreement to dispute; 2024 models

  • Devin Fusion: Frontier Performance at 60% Lower Cost
    Cognition, June 2026; company blog

    A router pairs a top model with a cheap "sidekick" and switches between them during a session: 63.1 points on FrontierCode 1.1 Extended at $1.35 per task

  • From Simulation to Enaction: Post-trained language models recognize and react to their own generations
    Asvin G, Lindsey (IAS Princeton, Anthropic), May 2026; preprint

    Post-trained models recognize their own texts: entropy on their own generations is 3–4 times lower than on other models' texts; the link to swarm echo is the review's inference, not the authors'

Chapter 9 of 11

Collective beliefs: consensus, conformity, splits

How does a group of agents arrive at a shared belief? Unanimity among copies emerges when conformity times the number of interlocutors outweighs sampling randomness, and group size changes the kind of error without removing it; the evidence comes from small groups and depends on the models and experimental settings, and some effects depend on prompt formatting.

Copies of one model can lock onto a single answer, split into two camps, or follow a majority the model would not have chosen alone. The open question is which of those is happening, and whether the shared answer is right. Four lines of evidence are in this chapter. A toy swarm with a known correct answer, the Flag Game, allows a targeted intervention and shows how a change spreads through the group. Statistical-physics models link conformity, the number of interlocutors and sampling randomness. Majority and convention maps show when a population of copies settles on a shared norm, and what share of a stubborn minority it takes to overturn it. Probes read, inside the receiving model, traces that never appear in the messages. The last section asks how much of the "society" literature is the dynamics under study and how much is the design of the experiment.

Chapter glossary: temperature, conformity, phase transition

Temperature here is the sampling temperature of the copies: near zero they answer almost alike, and as it rises their answers scatter. Conformity (λ) is how strongly an agent moves toward answers already on the table. This is not Toby Ord's λ from Ch. 4, where λ measures how well work divides among the agents of a swarm. Majority strength (β) is the same quantity in models without an own bias. Own bias (h) is where the model leans when the opinions around it are split evenly. A phase transition is an abrupt change of regime: while conformity times the number of interlocutors is smaller than the noise, the group's answers stay mixed; once it outweighs the noise, the group locks onto one answer. In finite groups the transition is smoothed out, so the authors speak of a "crossover". A probe is a simple model that reads an agent's internal activations. Patching swaps one agent's evidence or activations for ones taken from elsewhere, to test what exactly influenced the group's answer.

The Flag Game: a toy swarm with a known answer

Reading a swarm with the methods of neural-network interpretability — probes, patching, and a search for the circuits through which one agent's belief changes another's answer — needs an experiment with a known ground truth and a way to intervene precisely. Elizabeth Pavlova and Hidenori Tanaka put that requirement in the title of Flag Game: A Toy Model for Mechanistic Swarm Interpretability. The July OpenAI and Hugging Face incident (Ch. 3), in which a false belief about the scorer spread through a message board the agents had set up for themselves, is what the paper says it is answering.

experiment with LLM agentsbeliefs & consensusecho & diversitysafetyUp to 128 agents in one game

Flag Game: A Toy Model for Mechanistic Swarm Interpretability

NTT Research; Harvard University; Cambridge Boston Alignment Initiative · Sep 2026

Toy swarm with a known answer: a GPT-4o team's accuracy peaks at ≈0.63 with 16 agents and rises no further; in larger groups, wrong consensus gives way to a split into two camps.

×
Fig. 9.1original ↗
More about this work
Authors
Elizabeth Pavlova, Hidenori Tanaka
Organizations
NTT Research; Harvard University; Cambridge Boston Alignment Initiative
Published
16 Sep 2026
Status
arXiv preprint
Source
arxiv.org/abs/2609.19124

Elizabeth Pavlova and Hidenori Tanaka (NTT Research and Harvard; preprint, 16 September 2026) built the toy so that the swarm would have a known correct answer. Each agent sees only a crop of the flag of one of 28 countries and exchanges guesses in pairs, through a shared board, or through a manager who cannot see the crops. The runs vary the number of agents from 4 to 128, the wording of how much to trust peers, the mix of GPT-4o and GPT-5.4, and the communication protocol. Separately, they swap one agent's crop and follow the change through the group.

More agents are not monotonically better, and what changes sharply is the kind of error.

  • With all GPT-4o agents and pairwise exchange, accuracy is ≈0.54 with 4 agents, peaks at ≈0.63 with 16, then ≈0.59 at 64–128 (40 seeds per point, ≈38 valid). There are no significance tests, and the step from 0.63 to 0.59 is small against the shaded band on the plot.
  • "Wrong consensus" occurs in about 26% of runs with 4 agents and in 0% with 32 and 128. A split into two camps rises from about 24% with 4 agents to about 60% with 128. In the authors' reading, the greater danger is not the split but unanimity under group pressure: the small group is confidently wrong together.
  • A single prompt line on trusting peers raises accuracy on the shared board from 0.54 to 0.81. Under pairwise exchange the optimum is α = 0.75.
  • Mixed GPT-4o + GPT-5.4 teams reach ≈0.70 versus ≈0.55 for an all-GPT-4o team and ≈0.64 for an all-GPT-5.4 team. The models miss differently: in 100 probes, GPT-5.4 named a country incompatible with its crop 18 times, GPT-4o 5 times. Group size was varied only for GPT-4o. GPT-5.4 was tested only with 8 agents.
  • Patching the crop of the most influential agent in one selected game raised that group's accuracy from 25% to 100%. The same fraction of agents, 1/8, gives on average +40% with 8 agents and about +17% with 128, so the same intervention loses force as the swarm grows. On the shared board, the outcome of the discussion roughly equals a simple majority vote over the initial guesses.

The communication structure in all of these runs is imposed from outside. The task is a toy, and the authors warn that the conclusions should not be transferred to deployed swarms. Inside that imposed setup, team composition and the shape of the exchange still move the outcome at least as much as the choice of model.

Temperature, conformity and phase transition

In multi-agent debates, agents answer independently, then see the others' answers and revise their own. Three papers describe these dynamics with quantitative models. Maya Okawa (NTT Research, Harvard) gives a theory of conformity and temperature in Emergence of Biased Consensus in Multi-Agent LLM Debates. A Stanford group fits an Ising-type model on thousands of communities in Physics of Agents: Statistical Mechanics Predicts Collective Behavior of AI Agents. A Göttingen group, in Disentangling interaction and bias effects in opinion dynamics of large language models, splits pairwise opinion shifts into the partner's influence and the model's pull toward its own position. A fourth paper, Consensus and Factual Dynamics in Large Populations of Interacting Language Models (Ricco, Onofri, Sammartino and Di Pietro), runs the same dynamics on populations of up to 1,000 copies on lattices and separately measures factual correctness.

experiment with LLM agentsbeliefs & consensusecho & diversitysafety11 agents in the main group experiment

Emergence of Biased Consensus in Multi-Agent LLM Debates

NTT Research; Harvard University · Aug 2026

In debates among copies, a weak model bias becomes a near-unanimous decision when conformity times the number of interlocutors outweighs the sampling temperature (λρN/T ≳ 1); experiments use groups of 3–11 agents and pre-2026 models.

×
Fig. 9.2original ↗
×
Fig. 9.3original ↗
More about this work
Authors
Maya Okawa
Organizations
NTT Research; Harvard University
Published
3 Aug 2026
Status
ICML 2026 (peer-reviewed conference)
Source
arxiv.org/abs/2608.02827

Maya Okawa (NTT Research, Harvard; ICML 2026 poster) asks what a weak bias of a single model becomes once copies debate. On the realistic tasks, ten GPT-4.1 Nano "financial advisers" build a portfolio, and the bias is the share of US assets. Six GPT-3.5 or GPT-4 "judges" pick the better answer on 148 MT-Bench questions, and the bias is a preference for "their own" answers. The synthetic tasks are a choice between neutral symbols, or between Jane and John, where any agreement is already a bias. The control knob is sampling temperature.

Low temperature turns a weak bias into unanimity, and the theory names when that happens.

  • At temperatures up to ≈0.6 the US share of the portfolio is ≈0.97, and at 1.1 and above ≈0.85. Market-adjusted return is worse at low temperature (≈1.05 versus ≈1.37). For GPT-3.5 judges, self-preference is ≈1.0 at T = 0 and ≈0.80 at 1.2 and above, and agreement with human labels is ≈0.855 at low temperature and ≈0.89 at high temperature. Identical copies at one low temperature answer alike, so a shared lean is amplified.
  • A collective norm emerges when conformity λ times the number of interlocutors ρN outweighs the temperature (λρN/T ≳ 1). In finite groups the transition is smoothed into a crossover and becomes sharper as N grows. Phase diagrams for GPT-4.1 Nano and Llama 3.1 405B follow the prediction: for Llama, consensus is ≈0.98 at T ≤ 0.3 and ≈0.38 at 1.5. The phase transition is shown rigorously only in the theory. The experiments use groups of 3–11 agents and pre-2026 models: no Claude, no Gemini, no GPT-5-class model.
  • From the debate transcripts, each of 11 models received a profile of conformity and bias. DeepSeek V3 conforms most strongly, while the GPT-4.1 family has negative conformity. GPT-4.1 Nano is the sharp case: its conformity is negative, and its unanimity rests on the identical bias of the copies, not on peer pressure. Mixing models in triads, and mixing temperatures, weakens the bias, but only modestly in the realistic tasks (temperature mix in the portfolio task: bias 0.918 → 0.898).

The same derivation is where a dense connection is exposed: the more interlocutors, the lower the threshold at which a weak lean locks in. The levers that follow are more sampling randomness, sparser links, hidden neighbour confidence, and a mix of models. They are levers inside this theory and these group sizes.

experiment with LLM agentsbeliefs & consensusecho & diversitytheory64 agents in an additional mixed community; 32 in the main experiment

Physics of Agents: Statistical Mechanics Predicts Collective Behavior of AI Agents

Stanford University; UC Santa Barbara · Aug 2026

Groups of 32 copies move from indifference to consensus or polarization within eight rounds, and an Ising-type model predicts an agent's next opinion with 75–86 balanced accuracy.

More
Authors
Batu El, Jinhee Paeng, Fatih Dinc, Shiye Su, Mete Erdogan et al. (10 authors)
Organizations
Stanford University; UC Santa Barbara
Published
17 Aug 2026
Status
arXiv preprint
Source
arxiv.org/abs/2608.16578

Batu El, Jinhee Paeng and colleagues (Stanford; senior authors Ganguli and Zou; preprint, August 2026), in Physics of Agents: Statistical Mechanics Predicts Collective Behavior of AI Agents, run about 10,000 communities of 32 agents through 8 rounds of short messages over a graph, on MATH problems and political statements. The models are GPT-4o-mini, Gemma-3n-E4B, Qwen3.5-9B and Llama-3.1-8B. An Ising-type model with three coupling coefficients predicts an agent's next opinion with 75–86 balanced accuracy. The fitted parameters place the groups below the critical "social temperature": conviction grows in every condition, and groups move from indifference to consensus or polarization. On mathematics, a wrong majority becomes right more often than the reverse (GPT-4o-mini: 28% versus 11% of groups), while on politics three models out of four drift to the right (for Gemma, the share of groups with a right-leaning majority rises from 75% to 96%). The echo takes hold even when agents have different personas. The votes are answers without reasoning, each group uses a single model, and GPT-5.6-sol and DeepSeek-V4-Flash were tested only as a check of the predictor.

Disentangling interaction and bias effects in opinion dynamics of large language models

Max Planck Institute for Dynamics and Self-Organization; University of Göttingen · Sep 2026

The "agreement" of two copies is largely the model's prior opinion rather than the outcome of an exchange of arguments.

More
Authors
Vincent C. Brockers, David A. Ehrlich, Viola Priesemann
Organizations
Max Planck Institute for Dynamics and Self-Organization; University of Göttingen
Published
22 Sep 2026
Status
Nature Communications (peer-reviewed journal)
Source
doi.org/10.1038/s41467-026-77340-3

Vincent C. Brockers, David A. Ehrlich and Viola Priesemann (Max Planck Institute for Dynamics and Self-Organization and the University of Göttingen; Nature Communications, 22 September 2026) had two agents of the same model debate for five rounds on 12 topics. A Bayesian model splits opinion shifts into the partner's influence and three biases: toward the model's own position on the topic, toward agreement, and toward the first statement. For five of six models (Llama-3.1-8B, Qwen2.5-7B, two Mixtral variants, GPT-4o-mini), opinions converge within one or two rounds to a topic-dependent attractor; the sixth, Grok-4.1-fast, shows no convergence. Topic bias has the greatest standalone explanatory power for all six models. In GPT-4o-mini and Grok, however, the interaction effect tends to exceed the topic-bias effect in strength. The partner’s influence decays within a few rounds. Fine-tuning on climate messages shifts the attractor (Mixtral). The measurements are pairs only, on small models without reasoning, across these 12 topics. The paper is Disentangling interaction and bias effects in opinion dynamics of large language models.

Consensus and Factual Dynamics in Large Populations of Interacting Language Models

KAUST, University of Pisa · Sep 2026

In populations of up to 1,000 copies of one model, answers quickly converge in meaning, but consensus does not guarantee factual correctness: on average the exchange repaired answers more often than it damaged them, yet in 85 of 1,800 runs the hallucination share rose markedly.

More
Authors
Emanuele Ricco, Elia Onofri, Vincenzo Sammartino, Roberto Di Pietro
Organizations
KAUST, University of Pisa
Published
30 Sep 2026
Source
arxiv.org/abs/2609.39211

Ricco, Onofri, Sammartino and Di Pietro (KAUST; Sammartino also University of Pisa; preprint, 30 September 2026), in Consensus and Factual Dynamics in Large Populations of Interacting Language Models, place copies of one frozen Qwen3-14B model (4-bit) on lattices of different geometry: a ring of 8, 16 and 32 agents (2 neighbours), a 2D torus of 64, 144 and 256 (4 neighbours), a 3D torus of 64, 216, 512 and 1,000 (6 neighbours) and an all-to-all graph of 32 and 64. Agents first answer independently, then rewrite their answers in random sequential order, reading their neighbours and their own previous answer: 10 sweeps, up to 100 new tokens per response. 6 FreshQA questions × 12 size/geometry settings × 6 temperatures give 432 configurations with 5 trials each, 2,160 runs in total; the 4,767,840 model calls count responses, not population size. - Meaning and facts diverge. Semantic cohesion increases almost everywhere. On five questions the exchange repaired answers more often than it damaged them on average, but in 85 of 1,800 runs the hallucination fraction rose by more than 0.1 — this is not a benchmark quality score. - Judge validation. Human validation showed that on the false-premise question the judge systematically inverted its verdicts; the authors excluded that question from the pooled factual analysis while keeping the data. - Temperature and timing. The optimal temperature depends on what the authors call model coupling, and most of the shift happens in the first sweeps; these are the authors' conclusions within 10 sweeps, not general operating rules.

The runs use one model. The Qwen2.5-72B judge sees the question and the answer but not the gold answer. In the broad comparison, population size is partly confounded with geometry; the narrower comparisons hold N = 64 fixed. The ≈$540 figure is a hypothetical cloud-tariff estimate, not a bill (the campaign ran about 15 days on two RTX A6000 GPUs). The licence is CC BY-NC-SA 4.0.

Majority strength: maps of conventions and tipping points

Another branch of the same physics looks not at debates but at pure coordination: a population of copies chooses one of two words while seeing the others' choices. The quantities measured are majority strength β, the critical group size above which consensus is no longer reached, and the share of a stubborn minority that can overturn an established norm. Two of these works appeared in Science Advances and PNAS in 2026 and two are 2026 preprints, but all experiments use models from 2023–2025. The last card in this section (Banerjee and Moghaddas) is not a word game but the spread of a belief across networks of 30 agents on two small open models.

experiment with LLM agentsbeliefs & consensustheoryUp to 1,000 agents in the model population; one updates per step

AI agents can coordinate via majority-following beyond human scale

University of Konstanz; Centro Ricerche Enrico Fermi; Complexity Science Hub; ISC-CNR · Sep 2024

The more capable the model, the more strongly its copies follow the majority; the critical group size up to which copies reach consensus is about 30 for Llama 3 70B and about 1,000 for GPT-4 Turbo.

More
Authors
Giordano De Marzo, Claudio Castellano, David Garcia
Organizations
University of Konstanz; Centro Ricerche Enrico Fermi; Complexity Science Hub; ISC-CNR
Published
4 Sep 2024
Status
Science Advances (peer-reviewed journal)
Source
doi.org/10.1126/sciadv.aea6091

De Marzo, Castellano and Garcia (Konstanz, Rome; Science Advances, 14 August 2026), in AI agents can coordinate via majority-following beyond human scale, measured how a population of copies of one model chooses between the meaningless labels k and z while seeing the others' answers. The probability of joining the majority falls on a single curve P(m) = ½[tanh(βm) + 1] with majority strength β (the authors' "majority force"). Critical group size: about 30 for Llama 3 70B, about 80 for GPT-4o, about 1,000 for GPT-4 Turbo; for Claude 3.5 Sonnet, majority strength at N = 1,000 is still above the threshold, so this is a lower bound. The correlation of MMLU with majority strength β is 0.79 (p = 0.006), and with log N_c 0.82 (p = 0.013); for N_c, the authors themselves say the relation with most other benchmarks is not significant. This is the echo of copies in pure form, but on snapshots of 2023–2024 models, with no correct answer and no model mixing; the N = 1,000 point for GPT-4 Turbo is a single run.

Conformity Generates Collective Misalignment in AI Agents Societies

Univ. of Konstanz; CREF; Complexity Science Hub; Sapienza Univ. of Rome; MPI-DS Göttingen · May 2026

A majority of copies can get stuck against the model's own bias: more than 60% of opinion pairs do so for Gemma 3 27B, and the share depends strongly on the model.

More
Authors
Giordano De Marzo, Alessandro Bellina, Claudio Castellano, Viola Priesemann, David Garcia
Organizations
Univ. of Konstanz; CREF; Complexity Science Hub; Sapienza Univ. of Rome; MPI-DS Göttingen

arxiv.org/abs/2605.10721

The same authors, joined by Bellina and Priesemann (preprint, 11 May 2026), in Conformity Generates Collective Misalignment in AI Agents Societies, added an own bias h to the formula (the model's leaning toward one of two meaningful slogans) and searched for this trap (β > 1) with 50 copies and 100 opinion pairs across nine models: for Gemma 3 27B it holds more than 60% of pairs, for gemini-2.5-flash-lite 60%, for gpt-5-mini 30%. Adding 35 stubborn agents to 50 regular ones flips the pair on gender self-identification so that the population stays on the new side until the end of observation even after they leave, while 225 fail to lock in the pair on renewable energy. Agreement among copies is not the same as the model's preference on its own. Here h is a pull toward a slogan, not refusal of a harmful request. Reasoning is off or minimal, and the social signal is written into the prompt as a list.

Group size effects and collective misalignment in LLM multi-agent systems

City St George's, University of London; IT University of Copenhagen; UPC · Oct 2025

As a population of copies grows, the outcome becomes deterministic: for GPT on the White/African pair, the share of the strong word rises from 0.599 at N = 3 to 1.00 at N = 1,000.

Authors
Ariel Flint, Luca Maria Aiello, Romualdo Pastor-Satorras, Andrea Baronchelli
Organizations

Published

Flint, Aiello, Pastor-Satorras and Baronchelli (London, Copenhagen, Barcelona; PNAS, August 2026), in Group size effects and collective misalignment in LLM multi-agent systems, study homogeneous populations of Phi-4, GPT-4o, QwQ-32B and Llama 3.1 70B in a naming game: a random pair chooses one of two socially loaded words. At N = 24, interaction amplifies an individual bias, creates one from a neutral start, or reverses it, and the direction depends on the model: on the her/his pair, Qwen and Phi converge on her, GPT and Llama on his, from almost the same individual tendency. As N grows, the outcome becomes deterministic (the White/African pair for GPT: 0.599 at N = 3, 1.00 at N = 1,000). Large populations are computed from pre-extracted policies, the models date from 2024–2025, and the authors state explicitly that this is not misalignment with human values.
